On the cohomology of p p -adic analytic spaces, II: The C st C_{\operatorname{st}} -conjecture Thanks: The authors’ research was supported in part by the grant NR-19-CE40-0015-02 COLOSS and the NSF grant No. DMS-1440140.
Pierre Colmez, Wiesława Nizioł

Abstract
. Long ago, Fontaine formulated conjectures (now theorems) relating étale and de Rham cohomologies of algebraic varieties over p p -adic fields. In an earlier work we have shown that pro-étale and de Rham cohomologies of analytic varieties in the two extreme cases (proper and Stein) are also related. In the proper case, the comparison theorems are similar to those for algebraic varieties, but for Stein varieties they are quite different. In this paper, we state analogs of Fontaine’s conjectures for general smooth dagger varieties, that interpolate between these two extreme cases, and we prove them for many “small” varieties (cases we deal with include products of overconvergent affinoids and proper varieties, analytifications of algebraic varieties, or "almost proper" dagger varieties). The proofs use a “geometrization” of all involved cohomologies in terms of quasi-Banach-Colmez spaces (qBC’s for short, quasi- because we relax the finiteness conditions). The heart of the proof relies on delicate properties of BC’s and qBC’s. These properties should be of independent interest and we have devoted a large part of the paper to them.



1.
Introduction

Let
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
be a complete discrete valuation ring with fraction field

K
of characteristic 0 and with perfect
residue field
k
, countable, of characteristic
p
.
Let
\overline{K}
be an algebraic closure of
K
,
and let
{\mathscr{O}}_{\overline{K}}
denote the integral closure of
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
in
\overline{K}
.
Let
{\mathscr{O}}_{C}
be the
p
-adic completion of
{\mathscr{O}}_{\overline{K}}
and let
C
be its
field of fractions
(i.e.,
C
is the completion of
\overline{K}
; note that
{\mathscr{O}}_{C}/p\simeq{\mathscr{O}}_{\overline{K}}/p

is countable by our assumption on
k
).
Let
W(k)
be the ring of Witt vectors of
k
with
fraction field
F
(i.e,
W(k)={\mathscr{O}}_{F}
) and
let
\varphi
be the absolute
Frobenius on
W(\overline{k})
. Set
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}=\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{K}/K)
.

1.1.
Comparison theorems for algebraic varieties

In order to put our results into perspective, let us first recall what is known for algebraic varieties.
The story started with Tate conjecturing
[
48
]
the existence of a Hodge-like decomposition for
the étale cohomology of smooth and proper algebraic (or even rigid analytic) varieties over
K
and
proving the existence of such a decomposition for abelian varieties with good reduction.
One upshot of Tate’s results is that the
p
-adic periods of algebraic varieties do not live in
C
.
Fontained constructed
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
rings
{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}
,
{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
,
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
that should contain
these periods and refined Tate’s conjecture by conjecturing (first
[
24
,
25
]
in the case of
X
with good
reduction and then
[
27
]

in the case of
X
with semistable reduction) the
existence of functorial period isomorphisms relating étale and de Rham cohomologies of smooth algebraic varieties.

Conjecture 1.1
.

(Fontaine)

Let
X
be a proper and smooth algebraic variety over
K
admitting
a semistable model over
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
. Let
i\geq 0
.

(i)

(Conjecture
C_{\operatorname{dR}}
)

We have a functorial
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant isomorphism preserving filtrations

H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}\simeq H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}

(ii)

(Conjecture
C_{\operatorname{st}}
)

We have a functorial
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant isomorphism commuting with
\varphi
and
N

H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}\simeq H^{i}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\otimes_{F}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}},

compatible with the de Rham period morphism, and the natural injections

{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}{\subset}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
and
H^{i}_{\rm HK}{\subset}H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}
.

As we explain below (Remark
1.3
) these conjectures are now
theorems (even without the assumptions on existence of nice models, properness, or even smoothness).

Remark 1.2
.

(i) All the above cohomology groups are finite dimensional over the appropriate field
(
{\bf Q}_{p}
for étale cohomology,
K
for de Rham, and
F
for Hyodo-Kato).

(ii)
The existence of the period isomorphism implies in particular that

\dim_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})=\dim_{K}H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X).

(iii) The Hyodo-Kato cohomology group in (ii) is an
F
-vector space with a semilinear
Frobenius
\varphi

and a monodromy operator
N
satisfying
N\varphi=p\varphi N
. Moreover, there is a Hyodo-Kato isomorphism

\iota_{\rm HK}:H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X)\otimes_{F}K\overset{\sim}{\to}H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)

(iv) In
C_{\operatorname{dR}}
, the filtration on the left-hand side is the one coming from
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
; the one on the
right-hand side is the tensor product of the Hodge filtration on
H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)
and the filtration
on
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
. In
C_{\operatorname{st}}
, the
\varphi
and
N
on the left-hand side are those coming from
{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
;
on the right-hand side they are the tensor products of those coming from
H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X)

and
{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
.

(v) Galois properties of the rings
{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
and
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
make it possible to recover
de Rham cohomology from étale cohomology by taking fixed points by
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
: we have
functorial “étale-to-de Rham” isomorphisms

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)\simeq(H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})^{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}},\quad{\text{as filtered $K$-vector spaces,}}

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X)\simeq(H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})^{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}},\quad{\text{as $F$-vector spaces with a $\varphi$ and a $N$}}.

Moreover, the Hydo-Kato isomorphism is induced by the inclusion
{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}\subset{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
.
Note that, instead of tensor products, we could have considered
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant homomorphisms:
we have
functorial isomorphisms

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{\boldsymbol{*}}\simeq{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}),{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}),\quad{\text{as filtered $K$-vector spaces,}}

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X)^{\boldsymbol{*}}\simeq{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}),{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}),\quad{\text{as $F$-vector spaces with a $\varphi$ and a $N$}}.

(vi) It is possible to extract
{\bf Q}_{p}
from
{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
, using the extra structures. This induces
a description of étale cohomology by de Rham cohomology (with the extra structures coming from
Hydo-Kato cohomology), i.e., it gives a “de Rham-to-étale” bicartesian diagram

\textstyle{H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X)\otimes_{F}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})^{N=0,\varphi=1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{0}(H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}}

One can refine this diagram by taking a large enough twist, which
makes it possible to remove denominators
1
1

1

Recall that
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}={\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}[\frac{1}{t}]
,

{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}={\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+}[\frac{1}{t}]
.
in
t
:
if
r\geq i
, we have a bicartesian diagram

\textstyle{H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X)\otimes_{F}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{r}(H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}

(vii) The pair
(H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X),H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X))
is a filtered
(\varphi,N)
-module

in the sense of Fontaine; the fact that the above diagram is bicartesian and
H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})

is finite dimensional implies, in particular,
that this filtered
(\varphi,N)
-module is
weakly admissible
, a condition that can be described
purely in terms of the interplay between
\varphi
,
N
and the filtration.

Remark 1.3
.

(i) As we have mentioned above, Fontaine’s conjecture is now a theorem. There have been essentially
four lines of attack: the almost étale approach
[
20
]
, the syntomic approach
[
49
]
,
the motivic approach
[
38
]
, and the derived geometry approach
[
3
]
.
The resulting period isomorphisms are compared in
[
39
]
.
The most comprehensive results are those of Beilinson
[
3
]
: there is
no assumption of properness, existence of good models or smoothness.

(ii)
If we don’t assume
X
to have a semistable model over
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
then
H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X)
has to be replaced with
H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X_{\overline{K}})
, which is not
an
F
-vector space anymore but a
F^{\rm nr}
-vector space, where
F^{\operatorname{nr}}
is the maximal unramified extension of
F
. Moreover,
H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X_{\overline{K}})
is equipped with a semi-linear action
of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
commuting with
\varphi
and
N
and the action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
is smooth (i.e., any element

x
is fixed by
{\mathscr{G}}_{L}
for some finite extension
L
of
K
that depends on
x
). In this case,
the isomorphisms involving
H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X)
in (v) of Remark
1.2
involve smooth
vectors for the action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
and not only fixed vectors: i.e, we have
a functorial isomorphism

H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X_{\overline{K}})^{\boldsymbol{*}}\simeq{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}^{\rm sm}(H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}),{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})\quad{\text{of $(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})$-modules over $F^{\rm nr}$}}.

1.2.
Analytic varieties

Interest in analytic varieties is more recent despite the fact that Tate
[
48
]

already formulated his conjecture for rigid analytic spaces. Scholze
[
43
]
established
a version of Tate’s original conjecture for
smooth and proper analytic spaces over
K
or
C

and proved the
C_{\rm dR}
-conjecture for smooth and proper analytic spaces over
K
. We
proved the
C_{\rm st}
-conjecture for
proper analytic spaces with a semistable model
[
16
]
(see
[
32
]
for a simplified construction) and “de Rham-to-pro-étale” versions of the
C_{\rm st}
-conjecture
for Stein varieties
[
13
]

over
K
(also with a semistable model).

In this paper, we will consider smooth dagger varieties
over
K
or
C
(without any assumption on the existence of nice models):

\bullet
Any proper or partially proper rigid analytic variety has a natural dagger structure, and this
includes, in particular, analytifications of algebraic varieties, Stein varieties (e.g. étale
coverings of Drinfeld’s spaces in any dimension), or, more generally, holomorphically convex varieties
(proper fibrations over a Stein base).

\bullet
Dagger varieties that are not necessarily partially proper include overconvergent affinoids
or, more generally, quasi-compact rigid analytic varieties with an overconvergent structure.

As we have seen in the case of algebraic varieties, Fontaine’s conjectures
C_{\operatorname{dR}}
and
C_{\operatorname{st}}
can
be split in two: a “de Rham-to-étale” direction, and a “étale-to-de Rham” direction.
We are going to state analogous conjectures for analytic varieties but with étale cohomology replaced
with pro-étale cohomology (i.e., we are dealing with “rational”
p
-adic Hodge theory and not
“integral”
p
-adic Hodge theory; in particular, the pro-étale cohomology of the analytication
of an algebraic variety is much bigger than the étale cohomology of the algebraic
variety – the latter is finite dimensional).

1.2.1.
The “de Rham-to-étale”
C_{\operatorname{dR}}
and
C_{\operatorname{st}}
conjectures for dagger varieties

Conjecture 1.4
.

(de Rham-to-pro-étale
C_{\operatorname{dR}}
+
C_{\operatorname{st}}
)

Let
X
be a smooth dagger variety over
C
. Let
i\leq r
.
We have a functorial bicartesian diagram:

\textstyle{H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}(F^{r}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\rm dR}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\rm dR}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})}

Remark 1.5
.

(i) The
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-cohomology group
H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X/{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})
is a torsion-free
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module from
which one recovers the usual de Rham cohomology by moding out by
t
. If
X
is defined over
K
then

H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{C}/{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})\simeq H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
.

(ii) If
X
is defined over
K
, all spaces are endowed with a natural topology
and an action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
and all maps
are supposed to be
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant and continuous.

(iii) As we have shown in
[
18
]
,
if
X
is defined over
C
, then all spaces have a natural topology
and are
C
-points of VS’s (pro-étale sheaves of
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces on the category

{\rm Perf}_{C}
of perfectoid spaces over
C
) and all maps are supposed to be evaluations
of morphisms of VS’s and continuous.

(iv) The Hyodo-Kato cohomology group

H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X)
(see
[
18
, Sec. 2]
) is a
F^{\operatorname{nr}}
-module with a Frobenius
\varphi
,
a monodromy operator
N
, a (pro-)smooth action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
, and a Hyodo-Kato isomorphism

\iota_{\rm HK}:H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}\overset{\sim}{\to}H^{i}_{\rm dR}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})
.
The definitions of
H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X)
and
\iota_{\rm HK}

are adapted from the ones of Beilinson in the algebraic setting and use the alterations
of Hartl and Temkin to produce good local models.

(v)
In the case of proper analytic varieties, all cohomology groups
in the diagram are finite dimensional (as was the case in the algebraic setting)
and the kernels of the horizontal arrows are
0
.
This is not the case for a general analytic variety and all spaces
have to be seen in the derived category of locally convex topological vector spaces over
{\bf Q}_{p}
; in particular,
the tensor products are (derived) completed tensor products.
Even if
H^{i}_{\rm dR}(X)
, for
X
over
K
, is finite
dimensional,
H^{i}({\rm Fil}^{r}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\widehat{\otimes}_{K}{{\rm R}\Gamma}_{\rm dR}(X)))

surjects onto
\Omega^{i}(X_{C})^{d=0}

and hence can be huge (and then so is
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(r))
).

1.2.2.
The “étale-to-de Rham”
C_{\operatorname{dR}}
and
C_{\operatorname{st}}
conjectures for dagger varieties

In the other direction, we have the following conjectures:

Conjecture 1.6
.

(pro-étale-to-de Rham)

Let
X
be a smooth dagger variety defined over
K
. We have functorial isomorphims:

\displaystyle(C_{\operatorname{dR}})

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)^{\boldsymbol{*}}\simeq{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}),{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}),\quad{\text{as filtered $K$-vector spaces}},

\displaystyle(C_{\operatorname{st}})

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X)^{\boldsymbol{*}}\simeq{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}^{\rm sm}(H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}),{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}),\quad{\text{as $(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})$-modules over $F^{\rm nr}$}}.

Remark 1.7
.

As we mentioned above, even if
H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)
is finite dimensional,
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})
is, in general,
huge. Hence it is a little bit of a miracle that one could recover
H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)
from it.

The previous conjecture uses Galois action to recover de Rham and Hyodo-Kato cohomologies from pro-étale
cohomology. If
X
is defined over
C
, there is no Galois action anymore but one can use the VS structure
alluded to above to recover part of the structure. This leads to the following conjecture:

Conjecture 1.8
.

Let
X
be a smooth dagger variety defined over
C
.
We have functorial isomorphisms:

\displaystyle({\mathbb{C}}_{\operatorname{dR}})

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X/{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}),{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})\simeq{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{H}}^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}),\quad{\text{as ${\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}$-modules}},

\displaystyle({\mathbb{C}}_{\operatorname{st}})

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}(H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X),{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})\simeq{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{H}}^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm st}}),\quad{\text{as ${\bf B}_{{\rm st}}$-modules}}.

Remark 1.9
.

(i) It is not possible to recover the filtration on
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-cohomology
just by looking at the cohomology level because the
t^{k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
’s are all isomorphic
as VS’s whereas the
t^{k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
’s are all distinct as
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-modules.

(ii) In the same way,
\varphi
and
N
disappear since

M=M^{N=0,\varphi=1}\otimes_{{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{\varphi=1}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}

if
M=M_{0}\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
, where
M_{0}
is a finite rank
(\varphi,N)
-module over
F^{\rm nr}
.

(iii)
One way to understand points (i) and (ii) of the remark is the following.
Conjecture
1.4
represents
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}
as the
H^{0}

of a quasi-coherent sheaf on the Fargues-Fontaine curve that is obtained as the "modification"
at
\infty
of the sheaf associated to a
\varphi
-module. The
H^{0}
determines the sheaf
(because there is no
H^{1}
), but not
the "modification" that gave rise to it. Maybe a more sophisticated
formulation on the level of derived categories would allow to do that (see Remark
9.9
) ?

1.2.3.
Results supporting the conjectures

To state our main result towards these conjectures, say that a smooth dagger variety
is
small
if its de Rham cohomology is finite dimensional, and that a small
(smooth dagger) variety
has de Rham slopes
\geq 0
if the slopes of the vector bundle
on the Fargues-Fontaine curve associated to the filtered
(\varphi,N)
-modules

(H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X),F^{0}H^{i}(X/{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}))
are
\geq 0
for all
i
(this includes in particular
the case when these filtered
(\varphi,N)
-modules are weakly admissible: the slopes of the
associated vector bundles are all
0
in this case).

Remark 1.10
.

(i) Small varieties include proper varieties, overconvergent affinoids, quasi-compact dagger varieties,
analytifications of
algebraic varieties, certain tubular neighbourhoods of subvarieties of proper varieties or complements of such
tubular neighbourhoods.

(ii) We conjecture that all small varieties have de Rham slopes
\geq 0
.
Small varieties with de Rham slopes
\geq 0
include
proper varieties, overconvergent affinoids, analytifications of
algebraic varieties, various complements of
tubular neighbourhoods of subvarieties of proper varieties. Moreover, a product of two
small varieties with de Rham slopes
\geq 0
is again
a small variety with de Rham slopes
\geq 0
.

(iii) It is conceivable that the above mentionned results
could be extended to show that all complements of
tubular neighbourhoods of subvarieties of proper varieties have
de Rham slopes
\geq 0
. If one is very optimistic, one can also imagine
that any quasi-compact dagger variety can be written as
the complement of a
tubular neighbourhood of a subvariety of a proper variety.

Theorem 1.11
.

If
X
is small and has de Rham slopes
\geq 0
, then:

\bullet
Conjecture
1.4
is true.

\bullet
If
X
is defined over
K
, then Conjecture
1.6
is true,
and if
X
is defined over
C
,
Conjecture
1.8
is true.

The VS’s in the diagram in Conjecture
1.4

have some finiteness properties: they are extensions of
finite Dimensional Vector Spaces (also known as Banach-Colmez spaces,
and referred to as BC’s in the rest
of the text) by torsion
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Modules. In particular, such objects
{\mathbb{W}}

(referred to as qBC’s, the “q” standing
for “quasi”) have a height
{\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}})\in{\bf Z}
(if
{\mathbb{W}}
is the qBC attached
to a finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space
W
, then
{\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}})=\dim_{{\bf Q}_{p}}W
).
We have the following result echoing (ii) of Remark
1.2
;
it is a little bit surprising
that the pro-étale cohomology encodes this finiteness result considering how big it is
if
X
is not proper.

Theorem 1.12
.

If
X
is small with de Rham slopes
\geq 0
, then

{\rm ht}({\mathbb{H}}^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}))=\dim_{C}(H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{C})).

Remark 1.13
.

(i) The proof of Theorem
1.11
makes heavy use of the theory of BC’s and, in particular,
the geometric point of view advocated in Le Bras’s thesis
[
37
]
. About half of the paper
is devoted to proving results about BC’s that are needed in the proof of our main result.
Some of these results may be of independent interest.

(ii) For proper varieties, we can use the more naive theory of BC’s
as in
[
16
]
,
where we treated the case of varieties with semistable models over the integers.

(iii) For an overconvergent affinoid or a small Stein variety, one gets a direct proof from
the basic comparison theorem proved in
[
18
]
.

Remark 1.14
.

If
X
is smooth and Stein over
C
or
K
,
it can be written as a strict increasing union of overconvergent affinoids.
We get that
the horizontal rows in the diagram in Conjecture
1.4
are surjective and their kernels are

(\Omega^{i-1}(X)/{\rm Ker}\,d)(r-i)
. In particular, we have an exact sequence

0\to\Omega^{r-1}(X)/{\rm Ker}\,d\to H^{r}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\to(H^{r}_{\rm HK}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}\to 0

generalizing the exact sequence of
[
13
, Th. 1.8]
which was proven under the assumption that
X
has
a semistable model. It is not difficult to deduce Conjectures
1.4
,
1.6
and
1.8

in this case.

1.3.
Proofs

The main ingredients in the proofs are the results from
[
18
]
and the parallel theories
of BC’s
[
10
,
11
,
37
]
and Fontaine’s almost
C
-representations
[
29
,
30
]
.

Let
X
be a quasi-compact smooth dagger variety over
C
. From
[
18
]
we use the existence of the basic comparison isomorphism
with syntomic cohomology:

(1.15)

H^{i}_{\operatorname{syn}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r)),\quad i\leq r,

and the exact sequence

(1.16)

\cdots\to{\rm DR}_{r}^{i-1}(X)\to H^{i}_{\operatorname{syn}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\to{\rm HK}_{r}^{i}(X)\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\iota_{\operatorname{HK}}}}{\rm DR}_{r}^{i}(X)\to\cdots,

where we have set:

{\rm HK}_{r}^{i}(X):=(H^{i}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}},\quad{\rm DR}_{r}^{i}(X):=H^{i}({\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{r}).

Very important for what follows is that this exact sequence and the isomorphism (
1.15
) can be promoted to the category of VS’s
(see
[
18
, Th. 1.3, Th. 1.7]
).

1.3.1.
On the proof of Conjecture
1.4

Proving Conjecture
1.4
amounts to splitting the long exact sequence (
1.16
)
into short exact sequences,
which can be done directly for dagger affinoids (Theorem
6.14
),
going back to the definition of syntomic cohomology.
To treat the case of a small variety with de Rham slopes
\geq 0
,
we use the theory of BC’s. We already used BC’s in
[
16
]
to treat
the proper case (with a semistable model), but there we were helped by Scholze’s theorem
[
43
, Th. 1.1]
that
the
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))
’s are, in that case, finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces; this made
it possible to use the basic theory of BC’s as developed in
[
10
,
11
]
. In the present paper,
we need to use the more powerful point of view advocated in Le Bras’ thesis
[
37
]
.
The key result that comes out of the theory of BC’s is the following proposition
(Proposition
7.10
):

Proposition 1.17
.

The following properties are equivalent:

(a)
The diagram in Conjecture
1.4
is bicartesian.

(b)

The slopes of the vector bundle associated
to
(H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X),F^{0}H^{i}_{\rm dR}(X/{\bf B}_{\operatorname{dR}}))
are
\geq 0
, for
i=r-1
and
i=r
.

(c)
The kernel and cokernel of
{\mathbb{H}}^{r}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\to(H^{r}_{\rm HK}(X)\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}
have height
0
.

(d)

{\rm ht}({\mathbb{H}}^{r}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r)))=\dim_{C}H^{r}_{\rm dR}(X)
.

Among the ingredients that go into the proof of this proposition
are:

\bullet
an interpretation (Proposition
3.27
) of
{\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}})
for a qBC
{\mathbb{W}}
as
the rank of
{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})
(i.e. a
categorification
of the height
{\rm ht}
),

\bullet
results on
(\varphi,N)
-modules (Lemma
5.17
) and BC’s (Proposition
3.23
)
that tell us
what happens if the sequence associated to the diagram is not exact on the right.

It is in the application of Lemma
5.17

that we use in an essential way that the degrees of involved cohomology groups are
\leq r
:
this implies that the slopes of Frobenius on Hyodo-Kato cohomology are
\leq r
.

1.3.2.
On the proof of Conjectures
1.6
and
1.8

Conjectures
1.6
and
1.8
follow from Conjecture
1.4
and results
about almost
C
representations or BC’s such as:

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})=0\quad{\rm and}\quad{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})=0

(Proposition
2.14
for the first statement and Corollary
3.18
for the second;
this type of results allow us to get rid of the de Rham terms in the sequence of Hom’s
that is deduced from the exact sequence associated to the
bicartesian diagram of Conjecture
1.4
.) and, if
M
is a
(\varphi,N)
-module of slopes
\leq r
,

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}((M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})

\displaystyle=M^{\boldsymbol{*}}_{K}

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}((M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})

\displaystyle=M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}

(Theorem
5.8
and corollaries
for the first statement and Propositions
5.18
and
5.20
for the second).

.

1.3.3.
Notation and conventions.

Let
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
be a complete discrete valuation ring with fraction field

K
of characteristic 0 and perfect
residue field
k
of characteristic
p
. Let
\overline{K}
be an algebraic closure of
K
and let
{\mathscr{O}}_{\overline{K}}
denote the integral closure of
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
in
\overline{K}
. Let
C=\widehat{\overline{K}}
be the
p
-adic completion of
\overline{K}
. Let

W(k)
be the ring of Witt vectors of
k
with
fraction field
F
(i.e.,
W(k)={\mathscr{O}}_{F}
); let
e=e_{K}
be the ramification index of
K
over
F
. Set
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}=\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{K}/K)
and
let
\varphi
be the absolute
Frobenius on
W(\overline{k})
.
We will denote by
{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}},{\bf B}_{\operatorname{st}},{\bf B}_{\operatorname{dR}}

the crystalline, semistable, and de Rham period rings of Fontaine.

We will denote by
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
,

{\mathscr{O}}_{K}^{\times}
, and
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}^{0}
, depending on the context, the scheme
\operatorname{Spec}({{\mathscr{O}}_{K}})
or the formal scheme
\operatorname{Spf}({\mathscr{O}}_{K})
with the trivial, the canonical (i.e., associated to the closed point), and the induced by
{\bf N}\to{\mathscr{O}}_{K},1\mapsto 0
,
log-structure, respectively. Unless otherwise stated all formal schemes are
p
-adic, locally of finite type, and equidimensional. For a (
p
-adic formal) scheme
X
over
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
, let
X_{0}
denote
the special fiber of
X
; let
X_{n}
denote its reduction modulo
p^{n}
.

All rigid analytic spaces considered will be over
K
or
C
. We assume that they are separated, taut, and countable at infinity.

Our cohomology groups will be equipped with a canonical topology. To talk about it in a systematic way, we will work rationally in the category of locally convex
K
-vector spaces. For details the reader may consult
[
13
, Sec. 2.1, 2.3]
. To summarize quickly:
C_{K}
is the category of convex
K
-vector spaces;
it is a quasi-abelian category.
We will denote the left-bounded derived
\infty
-category of
C_{K}
by
{\mathscr{D}}(C_{K})
.
A morphism of complexes that is a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}(C_{K})
, i.e., its cone is strictly exact, will be called a
strict quasi-isomorphism
.
The associated cohomology objects are denoted by
\widetilde{H}^{n}(E)\in{\rm LH}(C_{K})
, where

{\rm LH}(C_{K})
is the left heart of
{\mathscr{D}}(C_{K})
;
they are called
classical
if the natural map
\widetilde{H}^{n}(E)\to{H}^{n}(E)
is an isomorphism.

Unless otherwise stated, we work in the derived (stable)
\infty
-category
{\mathscr{D}}(A)
of left-bounded complexes of a quasi-abelian category
A
(the latter will be clear from the context).
Many of our constructions will involve (pre)sheaves of objects from
{\mathscr{D}}(A)
.
We will use a shorthand for certain homotopy limits:
if
f:C\to C^{\prime}
is a map in the derived
\infty
-category of a quasi-abelian category, we set

[\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.93124pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.93124pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.29059pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.93124pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.93124pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C^{\prime}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces]:=\operatorname{holim}(C\to C^{\prime}\leftarrow 0).

For an operator
F
acting on
C
, we will use the brackets
[C]^{F=\lambda}
to denote the derived eigenspaces and the brackets
(C)^{F=\lambda}
or simply
C^{F=\lambda}
to denote the non-derived ones.

Finally, we will use freely the notation and results from
[
17
]
.

2.
Review of almost
C
-representations

We will briefly review Fontaine’s theory of almost
C
-representations

[
29
]
(see also
[
30
]
) and some of its consequences.
The theory has a satisfactory shape only when
[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]<\infty
, but some parts
work for
K
arbitrary. Fortunately, the almost
C
-representations that we are going
to deal with have special features and we are only going to use the results in
§
2.3
for which we provide alternative proofs (working for general
K
).

2.1.
Notation

A
banach
is a Banach space over
{\bf Q}_{p}
(up to an equivalence of norms) and a
banach representation
of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
is a banach with a continuous and linear action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
.
Denote by
{\mathscr{B}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
the category of banach representations of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
.
It has a natural exact category structure: a
short exact sequence
in
{\mathscr{B}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
is a sequence

0\to B_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}B_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\to}}B_{3}\to 0,

where
g
is a strict epimorphism and
f
is a
kernel of
g
(thanks to the open mapping theorem, this is equivalent to
f
,
g
being continuous and
the above sequence to be exact algebraically).

A
{\bf Q}_{p}
-representation of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
is a finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space with a continuous and linear action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
. Similarly, a
C
-representation of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
is a finite dimensional
C
-vector space with a continuous and semilinear action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
. We will denote by
\operatorname{Rep}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
, resp.
\operatorname{Rep}_{C}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
,
the category of
{\bf Q}_{p}
-representations, resp.
C
-representations. More generally, one can define the category
\operatorname{Rep}_{{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
of finite length
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-representations.

Fontaine proved the following surprising theorem:

Theorem 2.1
.

(Fontaine,
[
29
, Th.
A
, Th.
A^{\prime}
]
)

If
[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]<\infty
, the forgetful functors

\operatorname{Rep}_{C}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})\to{\mathscr{B}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K}),\quad\operatorname{Rep}_{{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})\to{\mathscr{B}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})

are fully faithful.

In other words, if
W_{1},W_{2}
are two
C
-representations of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
, all
{\bf Q}_{p}
-linear continuous
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant maps
W_{1}\to W_{2}
are necessarily
C
-linear. Similarly,
if
W_{1},W_{2}
are two
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-representations of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
, all
{\bf Q}_{p}
-linear continuous
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant maps
W_{1}\to W_{2}
are necessarily
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-linear.

Remark 2.2
.

The proof uses Sen’s theory
[
45
]
and gives a stronger result: one has the same statements
for
E
-linear maps between
E
-representations
for which the Hodge-Tate-Sen weights are algebraic over
E

(a condition that is automatic for
E={\bf Q}_{p}
, if
[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]<\infty
, whence the theorem).
In particular, we have the following fundamental result (where
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}
is
an abuse of notation for
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{B}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}
), valid for arbitrary
K
:

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(C,C)\simeq K

2.2.
Almost
C
-representations

2.2.1.
The general theory

Two banach representations
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
are
almost isomorphic
if there exist two finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces
V_{i}\subset W_{i}
,
i=1,2,
stable under
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
such that
W_{1}/V_{1}\simeq W_{2}/V_{2}
.
An
almost
C
-representation
is a banach representation which is almost isomorphic to
C^{d}
for some
d\in{\bf N}
.
Denote by
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
the category of almost
C
-representations.

Remark 2.3
.

The above definition makes sense for arbitrary
K
, but the theory has a satisfactory shape only
for
[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]<\infty
;
maybe the point of view developped in
[
30
]
would lead to a satisfactory theory for
arbitrary
K
?

From now on, assume that
[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]<\infty
. Then
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})

is an abelian subcategory of the exact category
{\mathscr{B}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
.
If
W/V_{2}\simeq C^{d}/V_{1}
,
one sets
\dim(W)=d
and
{\rm ht}(W)=\dim_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(V_{2})-\dim_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(V_{1})
.
This is independent of choices and yields additive functions
[
29
, Th. B]
-- a nontrivial fact whose proof uses the theory of BC’s
2
2

2

More specifically, the proof uses the result which says that,
if
S
is an effective BC of dimension
1
and height
h
and if
f:S\to C
is a morphism of BC’s whose image is not finite-dimensional, then
f
is surjective and its kernel has dimension
0
and height
h
.

[
10
]
,
[
11
]
.
We have
\dim(C)=1,{\rm ht}(C)=0
and
\dim(V)=0,{\rm ht}(V)=\dim_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(V)
if
V
is a
{\bf Q}_{p}
-representation. The category
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
contains all
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-representations and if
W
is a
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-representation of length
d
then it is almost isomorphic to
C^{d}

[
29
, Th. C]
;
we have
\dim(W)=d
and
{\rm ht}(W)=0
. In particular, the
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-representations
C
and
C(1)
are almost isomorphic.

Remark 2.4
.

The category
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
modulo almost isomorphisms is semi-simple with a single isomorphism class of simple objects, the class of
C
.

Fontaine reduced the computation of
\operatorname{Ext}
-groups in the category
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
to
their computation in the categories
\operatorname{Rep}_{{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
and

\operatorname{Rep}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
via the following fact (which relies on
[
29
, Prop. 5.5]

and
[
29
, Prop. 5.6]
):

Proposition 2.5
.

(Fontaine,
[
29
, Prop. 6.4, Prop. 6.5]
)

(i)
Let
X,Y\in{\rm Rep}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
. Then we have a canonical isomorphism

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(X,Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(X,Y),\quad i\geq 0.

(ii)

Let
X,Y\in{\rm Rep}_{{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
. Then we have a canonical isomorphism

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(X,Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(X,Y),\quad i\geq 0.

He also proved the following result:

Theorem 2.6
.

(Fontaine,
[
29
, Th. 6.1, Prop. 6.8, Prop. 6.9]
)

Let
X,Y\in{\mathscr{C}}({{\mathscr{G}}_{K}})
.

(i)
The
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(X,Y)
have finite rank and are trivial for
i\geq 3
.

(ii)

\sum_{i=0}^{2}(-1)^{i}\dim_{{\bf Q}_{p}}\operatorname{Ext}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}^{i}(X,Y)=-[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]\,{\rm ht}(X)\,{\rm ht}(Y)
.

(iii)
There exists a natural trace map
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(X,X(1))\to{\bf Q}_{p}
and, for
0\leq i\leq 2
, the map

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(X,Y)\times\operatorname{Ext}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}^{2-i}(Y,X(1))\to\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(X,X(1))\to{\bf Q}_{p}

defines a perfect duality.

2.2.2.
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-representations

{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-representations are objects of
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
and we have a recipe for computing

{\rm Ext}
groups between these objects. We still assume
[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]<\infty
,
but the results below are valid in greater generality (see Remark
2.2
).

Let
\chi
be the cyclotomic character. Let
K_{\infty}\subset K(\mu_{p^{\infty}})

denote the cyclotomic
{\bf Z}_{p}
-extension of
K
.
Let
\gamma
be a topological generator of
\operatorname{Gal}(K_{\infty}/K)
.
We choose a sequence
\{\zeta_{p^{n}}\}_{n\geq 1}
of primitive
p^{n}
’th roots of unity
\zeta_{p^{n}},n\geq 1
, such that
\zeta_{p^{n+1}}^{p}=\zeta_{p^{n}}
. Let
t\in{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
be the uniformizer associated to
\{\zeta_{p^{n}}\}_{n\geq 1}
.
We will also use its twisted form
t^{\prime}:=t/\pi_{t}
defined in
[
29
, Sec. 2.1]
;
it is a uniformizer of
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
as well,
fixed by
{\rm Gal}(\overline{K}/K_{\infty})
whereas
t
is only fixed by
{\rm Gal}(\overline{K}/K(\mu_{p^{\infty}}))
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
W
be a
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-representation. The groups

{\rm Ext}^{i}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(C,W)
are computed by the complex

\textstyle{W_{(0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x\mapsto(t^{\prime}x,(\gamma-1)x)}
\textstyle{W_{(1)}\oplus W_{(0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(x,y)\mapsto(t^{\prime}y-(\chi^{-1}(\gamma)\gamma-1)x)}
\textstyle{W_{(1)},}

where
W_{(0)}
is the space of generalized invariants
3
3

3

The elements killed by

(g_{1}-1)\cdots(g_{r}-1)
for all
g_{1},\dots,g_{r}\in{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
, for
r
big enough.

and
W_{(1)}=t^{\prime}((t^{\prime})^{-1}W)_{(0)}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.5
, the natural map

(2.8)

{\rm Ext}^{i}_{{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(C,W)\to{\rm Ext}^{i}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(C,W)

from the
\operatorname{Ext}
-groups in the category of
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-representations is an isomorphism.
Our proposition is now
[
29
, Th. 2.14]
.
∎

We will state two simple consequences of the above proposition.

Corollary 2.9
.

If
N\geq 0
and
1\leq i<j
, then

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{i},t^{-N}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{j})=0\quad{\rm and}\quad{\rm Ext}^{1}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{i},t^{-N}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{j})=0.

Proof.

By devissage (and twisting by
\chi^{-j}
for
0\leq j\leq i-1
), we can reduce to the case
i=1
.
If
W=t^{-N}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{j}
, we have
W_{(0)}=K
and
W_{(1)}=Kt^{\prime}
,
and our result follows from Proposition
2.7
(multiplication by
t^{\prime}

induces an isomorphism of
W_{(0)}
with
W_{(1)}
and the other maps are identically zero,
and it follows that the complex in the proposition is acyclic).
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
j\in{\bf Z},k\geq 1
. Then

\displaystyle{\rm Ext}^{0}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k},C(j))\simeq\begin{cases}0&{\text{if $j\neq 0$}}\\
K&{\text{if $j=0,$}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle{\rm Ext}^{1}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k},C(j))\simeq\begin{cases}0&{\text{if $j\neq 0,k$}}\\
K&{\text{if $j=0,k,$}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle{\rm Ext}^{2}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k},C(j))\simeq\begin{cases}0&{\text{if $j\neq k$}}\\
K&{\text{if $j=k.$}}\end{cases}

Proof.

We will use Proposition
2.7
with
W=({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})(1-j)
as well as the duality between

{\rm Ext}^{i}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(C,W)
and
{\rm Ext}^{2-i}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(W,C(1))
from Theorem
2.6
.
We have
W_{(0)}=Kt^{j-1}(1-j)
if
1\leq j\leq k
,

W_{(0)}=0
if
j\leq 0
or
j\geq k+1
, and
W_{(1)}\simeq Kt^{\prime}t^{j-1}(1-j)
if
0\leq j\leq j-1
,

W_{(1)}=0
if
j\leq-1
or
j\geq k
. In the complex from
Proposition
2.7

computing the
\operatorname{Ext}
-groups
{\rm Ext}^{i}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(C,W)
, the only nonzero maps
are the multiplications by
t^{\prime}:W_{(0)}\to W_{(1)}
which are isomorphisms unless exactly one of the two groups is trivial
(i.e.,
j=0
or
j=k
).
Our result follows.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

Every non-trivial
extension of
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}
by
C(k)
is isomorphic to
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k+1}
.

Example 2.12
.

Extensions of Tate twists.

We have
{\rm Ext}^{1}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(C,C)\simeq K
(the
K
-vector space is generated by the class of

C\otimes({\bf Q}_{p}\oplus{\bf Q}_{p}\log t)
).
Since
{\rm Ext}^{0}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(C,C)\simeq K
by Remark
2.2
,
this implies that
{\rm Ext}^{2}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(C,C)=0
by Theorem
2.6
.
We also have, by duality,
{\rm Ext}^{1}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(C,C(1))\simeq K
(generated by the class of
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{2}
)
and
{\rm Ext}^{2}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(C,C(1))\simeq K
, and all the other
{\rm Ext}^{i}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(C,C(j))
are trivial.

2.3.
Morphisms of
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-representations

In this section,
K
is arbitrary. We are going to derive consequences of the following two
results which are valid for such
K
.
These are the results that we will use in the rest of the paper.

Proposition 2.13
.

(i)

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(C,C)\simeq K
.

(ii)

\overline{K}
is dense in
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
.

Proof.

Fontaine’s proof
[
28
]
of (i) works
for arbitrary
K
(the alternative proof in
[
31
]
, which uses class field theory, works
only for
[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]<\infty
). For (ii), see
[
12
]
.
∎

Proposition 2.14
.

We have

(i)

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(C,C)\simeq K
,
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(C,C(j))=0
if
j\neq 0
.

(ii)

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+},t^{-N}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})\simeq K

for
N\geq 0
,
k\geq 1
; compatibly in
k
.

(iii)

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})\simeq K
,
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})\simeq K
.

(iv)

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(t^{j}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})=0
,

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{\ell}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})=0
if
\ell>k
.

Proof.

The first claim of (i) is Fontaine’s theorem.
The second claim of (i) follows from the fact that, thanks to Tate’s theorem that
H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{L},C(j))=0

if
j\neq 0
and
L
is a finite extension of
K
,
C(j)
does not have elements on which
the action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
factors through a finite quotient;
hence
\lambda\in{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(C,C(j))
is identically
zero on
\overline{K}
, and thus on
C
by continuity and density of
\overline{K}
in
C
.

Let us prove (ii).
Any map
\lambda:{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\to t^{-N}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
sends
\overline{K}
to

\overline{K}
since elements of
\overline{K}
are the smooth vectors for the action
of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
in
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
and
t^{-N}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
(by Tate’s theorem recalled above).
By continuity, and density of
\overline{K}

in
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
one sees that
\lambda({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})\subset{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
.

Now, if
k=1
,
\lambda(t\overline{K})
is sent to
0
in
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t=C
since

C(-1)
does not have elements on which the action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
factors through a finite quotient.
But
t\overline{K}
is dense in
t{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
, hence
\lambda
factors through
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t=C
.
It follows from (i) that
\lambda
is the multiplication by
\kappa\in K
(composed with

{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\to C
).
Now, if
k\geq 1
, we can compose with
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}\to C
to deduce that
there exist
\kappa\in K
such that
\lambda-\kappa
has values in
t{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
.
But the image of
\overline{K}
by
\lambda-\kappa
is identically
0
for the same reasons as above;
hence
\lambda=\kappa
and we are done.

The first statement of (iii) follows from (ii) since

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})=\operatorname{lim}_{k}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})

For the second statement,
let
\lambda\in{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})
.
By the same arguments as above,
\lambda(\overline{K})\subset\overline{K}
; hence, by continuity,

\lambda({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})\subset{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
and the restriction of
\lambda
to
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
is the multiplication
by an element
\kappa
of
K
. But
\lambda_{N}
defined by
\lambda_{N}(x)=t^{N}\lambda(t^{-N}x)

also belongs to
{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})
and is the multiplication by
\kappa
on
t^{N}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
.
It follows that
\lambda_{N}
is the multiplication by
\kappa
on
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
, and
\lambda
is the multiplication
by
\kappa
on
t^{-N}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
.
Hence the second claim of (iii).

Finally, for (iv) we may assume
j=0
by twisting (if
\lambda\in{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(t^{j}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})
,
then
x\mapsto t^{-j}\lambda(t^{j}x)\in{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k-j}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})
).
Then the same arguments as for (ii) show that

\lambda({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})\subset{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
for the first claim,
and that
\theta\circ\lambda
is
\kappa\theta
for some

\kappa\in K
for both claims. One deduces that
\lambda=\kappa
on
\overline{K}
.
But we can find
a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in{\bf N}}
of elements of
\overline{K}
converging to
t^{k}

in
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
hence to
0
in
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}
, and continuity of

\lambda
implies that
0=\lambda(0)=\kappa t^{k}
. Hence
\kappa=0
(since

\ell>k
for the second claim), and
\lambda=0
,
which finishes the proof.
∎

3.
The category
\mathscr{BC}

In this chapter we recall the definition of BC’s
4
4

4

Often called Banach-Colmez spaces.
, i.e., the objects of
the category
\mathscr{BC}
, and study their properties.
In particular, we study the canonical filtration and its relation to the Harder-Narasimhan filtration.
Moreover, we will partially categorify height of BC’s and introduce a notion
of acyclic
(\varphi,N)
-modules that will play an important role later on in the paper.

3.1.
The category
\mathscr{BC}

We will discuss now basic properties
of the category
{\mathscr{B}C}
of BC’s and the canonical filtration of its objects.

3.1.1.
Definitions and basic properties

Recall
[
10
]
that a BC
5
5

5

Called in
[
10
]
”finite dimensional Banach Space”.

{\mathbb{W}}
is,
morally, a finite dimensional
C
-vector space
up to a finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space.
It has a Dimension
{\rm Dim}\,{\mathbb{W}}=(a,b)
, where
a=\dim{\mathbb{W}}\in{\bf N}
,
the
dimension of
{\mathbb{W}}
, is the dimension of the
C
-vector space
and
b={\rm ht}\,{\mathbb{W}}\in{\bf Z}
, the
height of
{\mathbb{W}}
,
is the dimension of the
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space.

More precisely, a
Vector Space
(VS for short)

{\mathbb{W}}
is a functor
\Lambda\mapsto{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)
from
sympathetic algebras
6
6

6

In
[
10
]
, these are defined as being
spectral and connected
C
-Banach algebras
\Lambda
such that
x\mapsto x^{p}
is surjective on

\{x,\ \|x-1\|_{\Lambda}<1\}
. We will denote by
{\mathscr{O}}_{\Lambda}
the unit ball in
\Lambda
. We are going to request additionally that the natural map

\Lambda\to{\mathscr{C}}({\rm Spm}(\Lambda)\to C)
is injective (here
{\rm Spm}(\Lambda)

is the set of continuous morphisms
\Lambda\to C
, with pointwise convergence topology),
a property that was taken for granted in the arguments of
[
10
]
, but which
fails for example if
\Lambda={\mathscr{O}}_{C^{\prime}}
and
C^{\prime}
is a strictly bigger complete algebraic field
than
C
, like its spherical closure (another possibility would be to choose a big
field
\widehat{C}
contaning
C
, algebraically closed and spherically complete,
with group of valuation
{\mathrm{R}}
, and to define
{\rm Spm}(\Lambda)

as the set of continuous morphisms
\Lambda\to\widehat{C}
). We are also going to restrict ourselves
to separable
\Lambda
’s (i.e., having a dense
C
-subspace of countable dimension), in order to be
able to use the Hahn-Banach theorem despite the fact that we are not assuming
C
to be
spherically complete. Since we have assumed
{\mathscr{O}}_{C}/p

to be countable, the sympathetic closure of a spectral and connected
C
-Banach algebra
that is separable is itself separable.

to
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces.
By definition, a sequence
0\to{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}\to 0
is exact
if and only if
0\to{\mathbb{W}}_{1}(\Lambda)\to{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}(\Lambda)\to 0

is exact for all
\Lambda
.

Trivial examples of VS’s are:

\bullet

finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces
V
:

\Lambda\mapsto V
,

\bullet

{\mathbb{V}}^{d}
, for
d\in{\bf N}
, with
{\mathbb{V}}^{d}(\Lambda)=\Lambda^{d}
, for
all
\Lambda
.

More interesting examples are provided by Fontaine’s rings:

\bullet

{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm cris}}^{+}
,
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm st}}^{+}
,
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
,
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm cris}}
,
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm st}}
,
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}
are naturally VS’s (and even Rings).

\bullet
If
m\geq 1
, then
{\mathbb{B}}_{m}:={\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{m}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
is a VS (and also a Ring).

\bullet
Let
h\geq 1
and
d\in{\bf Z}
. Then
{\mathbb{U}}_{h,d}=({\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm cris}}^{+})^{\varphi^{h}=p^{d}}
if
d\geq 0
,
and
{\mathbb{U}}_{h,d}={\mathbb{B}}_{-d}/{\bf Q}_{p^{h}}
if
d<0
, are VS’s.

A Vector Space
{\mathbb{W}}
is said to be finite Dimensional (a BC for short)
if it “is equal to
{\mathbb{V}}^{d}
, for some
d\in{\bf N}
, up to
finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces”:
there exist finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces

V_{1},V_{2}
and exact sequences

0\to V_{1}\to{\mathbb{Y}}\to{\mathbb{V}}^{d}\to 0,\quad 0\to V_{2}\to{\mathbb{Y}}\to{\mathbb{W}}\to 0,

so that
{\mathbb{W}}
is obtained from
{\mathbb{V}}^{d}
by “adding
V_{1}
and
moding out by
V_{2}
”.
Then

\dim{\mathbb{W}}=d\quad{\rm and}\quad{\rm ht}\,{\mathbb{W}}=\dim_{{\bf Q}_{p}}V_{1}-\dim_{{\bf Q}_{p}}V_{2}

Remark 3.1
.

(i) We are, in general, only interested in
W={\mathbb{W}}(C)
but, without
the extra structure, it would be impossible to speak of its Dimension (for example,
C
and
C\oplus{\bf Q}_{p}
are isomorphic as topological
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces).

(ii) The functor
{\mathbb{W}}\mapsto{\mathbb{W}}(C)

of
C
-points is faithful for BC’s (this follows from Proposition
3.2
).
Also, if
{\mathbb{W}}
is a BC, then
{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)
is a
{\bf Q}_{p}
-Banach, for all
\Lambda
, and
if
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
is a morphism of BC’s, then
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}(\Lambda)\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}(\Lambda)

is continuous and strict,
for all
\Lambda
, and an exact sequence
0\to{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{3}\to 0
of BC’s
induces a strictly exact sequence for all sympathetic
\Lambda
.

We quote
[
16
, Prop. 5.16]
:

Proposition 3.2
.

(i)
The Dimension of a BC is independent
of the choices made in its definition.

(ii)
If
f:{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
is a morphism of
BC’s, then
{\rm Ker}\,f
,
{\rm Coker}\,f
and
\operatorname{Im}\,f

are BC’s, and
we have

{\rm Dim}\,{\mathbb{W}}_{1}={\rm Dim}\,{\rm Ker}\,f+{\rm Dim}\,\operatorname{Im}\,f\quad{\rm and}\quad{\rm Dim}\,{\mathbb{W}}_{2}={\rm Dim}\,{\rm Coker}\,f+{\rm Dim}\,\operatorname{Im}\,f.

(iii)
If
\dim{\mathbb{W}}=0
, then
{\rm ht}\,{\mathbb{W}}\geq 0
.

(iv)
If
{\mathbb{W}}
has an increasing filtration such that the successive quotients
are
{\mathbb{V}}^{1}
, and if
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}
is a sub-BC of
{\mathbb{W}}
, then

{\rm ht}\,{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}\geq 0
.

We will denote by
\mathscr{BC}
the category of BC’s. It is an abelian category.

Example 3.3
.

The Spaces
{\mathbb{B}}_{m}

and
{\mathbb{U}}_{h,d}
defined above are BC’s. Their Dimensions are

{\rm Dim}\,{\mathbb{B}}_{m}=(m,0),\quad{\rm Dim}\,{\mathbb{U}}_{h,d}=\begin{cases}(d,h)&{\text{if $d\geq 0$,}}\\
(-d,-h)&{\text{if $d<0$.}}\end{cases}

3.1.2.
Canonical filtration

In his thesis
[
40
]
,
[
41
]
, Plût introduced a filtration on objects of
\mathscr{BC}
and
stated a number of results about this filtration. We will review them briefly here.

Remark 3.4
.

Most of these results can
be recovered from the relation of
\mathscr{BC}
to vector bundles
on the Fargues-Fontaine curve and the Harder-Narasimhan filtration studied in Le Bras’ thesis (see Section
3.2.4
).

Definition 3.5
.

(
Curvature
)
Let
{\mathbb{W}}\in\mathscr{BC}
. We say that

{\mathbb{W}}
has:

\bullet
curvature
>0
, if
{\rm Hom}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{V}}^{1})=0
,

\bullet
curvature
\geq 0
, if
{\rm Hom}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})=0
.

\bullet
curvature
=0
, or is
affine
, if it is a successive extension of
{\mathbb{V}}^{1}
,

\bullet
curvature
<0
, if it injects into
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{d}
(or, equivalently, into
({\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})^{d}
),

\bullet
curvature
\leq 0
, if it injects into a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module (i.e., a VS with an action of
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
),

Remark 3.6
.

(i) If
{\mathbb{W}}
has curvature
>0
(resp.
\geq 0
)
and
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}
has curvature
\leq 0
(resp.
<0
),
then
{\rm Hom}_{\mathscr{BC}}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime})=0
.

(ii)
A sub-VS of an VS with curvature
\leq 0
(resp.
<0
) has curvature
\leq 0
(resp.
<0
).

(iii)
A quotient of an VS with curvature
\geq 0
(resp.
>0
) has curvature
\geq 0
(resp.
>0
).

Proposition 3.7
.

(The canonical filtration)

Every
{\mathbb{W}}\in\mathscr{BC}
has a unique filtration, called the canonical filtration,

{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}\subset{\mathbb{W}}_{\geq 0}\subset{\mathbb{W}}

such that:

\bullet

{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}
has curvature
>0
,

\bullet

{\mathbb{W}}_{\geq 0}/{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}
has curvature
0
,

\bullet

{\mathbb{W}}/{\mathbb{W}}_{\geq 0}
has curvature
<0
.

Proof.

One defines
{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}
as the intersection of the kernels
of all morphisms
\alpha:{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{B}}_{m}
, for
m\geq 1
,
and
{\mathbb{W}}_{\geq 0}
as the intersection of the kernels of all morphisms

\alpha:{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}
. That
{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}
has curvature
>0
and

{\mathbb{W}}/{\mathbb{W}}_{\geq 0}
curvature
<0
are clear.
That
{\mathbb{W}}_{\geq 0}/{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}
has curvature
0
results from the description
of the canonical filtration in terms of the Harder-Narasimhan filtration (cf. section
3.2.8
).
∎

Remark 3.8
.

(i)
{\mathbb{W}}_{\leq 0:}={\mathbb{W}}/{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}
is the largest quotient of curvature
\leq 0

of
{\mathbb{W}}
.

(ii)
{\mathbb{W}}_{=0}:={\mathbb{W}}_{\geq 0}/{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}
is the largest
affine sub-VS of
{\mathbb{W}}_{\leq 0}
.

3.2.
The category
\mathscr{BC}
and coherent sheaves on the Fargues-Fontaine curve

The canonical filtration of BC’s is closely related to
the Harder-Narasimhan filtration defined, using the presentation of
\mathscr{BC}

via coherent sheaves on the Fargues-Fontaine curve, by Le Bras
[
37
]
. We will now explain this relation.

3.2.1.
The Fargues-Fontaine curve

The (algebraic) Fargues-Fontaine curve
X=X_{\rm FF}
is the projective scheme attached to the
graded
{\bf Q}_{p}
-algebra
\oplus_{d\geq 0}{\rm U}_{d}
,
where
{\rm U}_{d}=({\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+})^{\varphi=p^{d}}
.
The closed points of
X
are in bijection with the
{\bf Q}_{p}
-lines of
{\rm U}_{1}
;
if
x\in X
, we fix a basis
t_{x}
of the corresponding line.
The field
C
corresponds to a specific point
\infty
of
X
, and
t=t_{\infty}

is Fontaine’s
p
-adic
2\pi i
.
We denote by
C_{x}
the residue field at
x
; it is an algebraically close field,
complete for
v_{p}
,
and
C_{x}^{\flat}=C^{\flat}
, but
C_{x}
is not necessarily
[
35
]
isomorphic to
C
.
The residue field at
\infty
is
C
itself.

The completed local ring
\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{X,x}
at
x
is the ring
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}(C_{x})
, and
t_{x}

is a uniformizer (if
x=\infty
, then
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}(C_{x})={\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
).

3.2.2.
Harder-Narasimhan categories

A
Harder-Narasimhan category
is an exact category with two real valued functions
{\rm rk}
and
{\rm deg}

(rank and degree) – which are additive in short exact sequences – satisfying extra conditions (see
[
22
, 5.5.1]
,
[
1
]
).
This allows to define a slope function
\mu=\frac{{\rm deg}}{{\rm rk}}
taking values
in
{\bf R}\coprod\{\pm\infty\}
(endowed with the obvious ordering).

An object
{\mathscr{E}}
is
of slope
\lambda
if

\mu({\mathscr{E}})=\lambda
. It is
semistable

if

\mu({\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})\leq\mu({\mathscr{E}})

for all strict subobjects
{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathscr{E}}
. It is
stable

if

\mu({\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})<\mu({\mathscr{E}})

for all strict subobjects
{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathscr{E}}
.
Any object
{\mathscr{E}}
has a canonical decreasing filtration (the
Harder-Narasimhan filtration
)
by strict subobjects
{\mathscr{E}}^{\geq\lambda}
(with
{\mathscr{E}}^{\geq\lambda}\subset{\mathscr{E}}^{\geq\mu}
if

\lambda\geq\mu
), such that, if
{\mathscr{E}}^{>\lambda}=\cup_{\mu>\lambda}{\mathscr{E}}^{\geq\mu}
,
then
{\mathscr{E}}^{\geq\lambda}/{\mathscr{E}}^{>\lambda}
is semistable of slope
\lambda
.

We say that
{\mathscr{E}}
has slopes
\geq\lambda
(resp.
>\lambda
) if

{\mathscr{E}}={\mathscr{E}}^{\geq\lambda}
(resp.
{\mathscr{E}}={\mathscr{E}}^{>\lambda}
),
and has slopes
\leq\lambda
(resp.
<\lambda
)
if
{\mathscr{E}}^{>\lambda}=0
(resp.
{\mathscr{E}}^{\geq\lambda}=0
).

This has a number of consequences:

\bullet
If
{\mathscr{E}}_{1}
is of slopes
\geq\lambda
and
{\mathscr{E}}_{2}
is of slopes
<\lambda
,
then
{\rm Hom}({\mathscr{E}}_{1},{\mathscr{E}}_{2})=0
.

\bullet
A quotient of an object of slopes
\geq\lambda
has slopes
\geq\lambda
.

\bullet
A subobject of an object of slopes
\leq\lambda
has slopes
\leq\lambda
.

\bullet
Il
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}
, if
{\mathscr{E}}_{1}
is of slopes
\geq\lambda_{1}
and

{\mathscr{E}}_{2}
of slopes
\leq\lambda_{2}
, then an extension of
{\mathscr{E}}_{2}
by
{\mathscr{E}}_{1}

has slopes in
[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
.

3.2.3.
Vector bundles

To a vector bundle

{\mathscr{E}}
on
X
, one can attach
its rank
{\rm rk}({\mathscr{E}})\in{\bf N}
, its degree
\deg({\mathscr{E}})\in{\bf Z}
,
and its slope
\mu({\mathscr{E}})=\frac{\deg({\mathscr{E}})}{{\rm rk}({\mathscr{E}})}
.
These definitions can be extended to torsion coherent sheaves by additivity in short exact
sequences. In particular,
torsion sheaves
have rank
0
, degree
>0
and slope
+\infty
.
Endowed with
{\rm rk}
and
{\rm deg}
, the category
{\rm Coh}_{X}
of
coherent sheaves on
X
is a Harder-Narasimhan category.

The following result
[
22
, Th. 8.2.10]
is fundamental:

Theorem 3.9
.

(Fargues-Fontaine)

(i)
If
\lambda=\frac{d}{h}

(in lowest terms)
,
there exists, up to isomorphism, a unique stable vector bundle

{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)
of slope
\lambda
; its rank is
h
and its degree
d
.

(ii)
Every vector bundle
{\mathscr{E}}
on
X
is a direct sum

{\mathscr{E}}={\mathscr{O}}(\lambda_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda_{r}).

In particular the Harder-Narasimhan filtration splits
(non canonically)
.

(iii)
Every coherent sheaf
{\mathscr{E}}
on
X
is a direct sum

{\mathscr{E}}={\mathscr{O}}(\lambda_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda_{r})\oplus\big(\oplus_{x\in X}{\mathscr{F}}_{x}\big),

where
{\mathscr{F}}_{x}
is a torsion coherent sheaf, supported at
x
and zero for almost all
x
.

The
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{r}
above are
the slopes of
{\mathscr{E}}

(to which one has to add
+\infty

if one of the
{\mathscr{F}}_{x}
is non zero).

We have, by
[
22
, Prop. 8.2.3]
,

(3.10)

\displaystyle H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda))=({\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+})^{\varphi^{h}=p^{d}},\quad H^{1}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda))=0,\quad{\text{if $\lambda\geq 0$,}}

\displaystyle H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda))=0,\quad H^{1}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda))={\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/(t^{-d}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\oplus{\bf Q}_{p^{h}}),\quad{\text{if $\lambda<0$.}}

Note that
H^{i}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda))
, for
i=0,1
, is the space of

C
-points of a BC
{\mathbb{H}}^{i}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda))
,
and we have

\displaystyle{\rm Dim}({\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)))=(d,h),\quad{\rm Dim}({\mathbb{H}}^{1}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)))=0,\quad{\text{if $\lambda\geq 0$,}}

\displaystyle{\rm Dim}({\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)))=0,\quad{\rm Dim}({\mathbb{H}}^{1}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)))=(-d,-h),\quad{\text{if $\lambda<0$.}}

Note also that

{\mathbb{H}}^{i}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda))
, for
i=0,1
, clearly depends only on

C^{\flat}
if
\lambda\geq 0
; this is less clear when
\lambda<0
(since
t
depends on

C
) but it is still true.

Remark 3.11
.

It follows for the above formulas that

{\rm ht}({\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)))-{\rm ht}({\mathbb{H}}^{1}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)))=h,\quad{\text{for all $\lambda$.}}

By additivity, this implies the following Euler-Poincaré formula for any vector bundle
{\mathscr{E}}

on
X
:

{\rm ht}({\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{E}}))-{\rm ht}({\mathbb{H}}^{1}(X,{\mathscr{E}}))={\rm rk}\,{\mathscr{E}}

This extends to coherent sheaves on
X
.

3.2.4.
The category
{\rm Coh}_{X}^{-}

In his thesis, Le Bras shows (cf.
[
37
, Prop. 7.8]
) that,
if
{\mathbb{W}}
is a BC and if
{\mathbb{W}}^{\rm sh}
stands
for the sheaf on
{\rm Perf}_{C}
associated to
{\mathbb{W}}
, then

{\mathbb{W}}^{\rm sh}(\Lambda)={\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)
for any sympathetic algebra
\Lambda
.
He also proves that the functor
{\mathbb{W}}\mapsto{\mathbb{W}}^{\rm sh}
identifies
\mathscr{BC}

as the smallest sub-abelian category of the category of pro-étale sheaves
on
{\rm Perf}_{C}
, stable
by extensions, and containing
{\bf Q}_{p}
and
{\mathbb{V}}^{1}
; this gives
an efficient alternative definition of
\mathscr{BC}
.

We note
{\rm Coh}_{X}^{-}
the sub-category of
D^{b}({\rm Coh}_{X})

of complexes
{\mathscr{E}}_{\bullet}
such that
H^{i}({\mathscr{E}}_{\bullet})=0
if
i\neq-1,0
,

H^{-1}({\mathscr{E}}_{\bullet})
has slopes
<0
and
H^{0}({\mathscr{E}}_{\bullet})
has slopes
\geq 0
.
Any object of
{\rm Coh}_{X}^{-}
can be represented by a
complex
{\mathscr{E}}_{-1}\overset{0}{\to}{\mathscr{E}}_{0}
of coherent sheaves such that

H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{E}}_{-1})=0
(i.e.,
{\mathscr{E}}_{-1}
has slopes
<0
) and
H^{1}(X,{\mathscr{E}}_{0})=0

(i.e.,
{\mathscr{E}}_{0}
has slopes
\geq 0
).
Le Bras defines an exact functor

{\rm BC}:{\rm Coh}_{X}^{-}\to{\mathscr{BC}}

(denoted by
R^{0}\tau_{\boldsymbol{*}}
in
[
37
, 6.2]
): for a complex of coherent sheaves
{\mathscr{F}}
on
X
,
{\rm{BC}}({\mathscr{F}})
is the sheaf associated to the presheaf sending
S
to the hypercohomology in degree
0

of
X_{S}
with values in
{\mathscr{F}}_{S}
.
By definition of this functor
7
7

7

More generally,

{\rm BC}({\mathscr{E}}_{\bullet})
is an extension of
{\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,H^{0}({\mathscr{E}}^{\bullet}))
by

{\mathbb{H}}^{1}(X,H^{-1}({\mathscr{E}}^{\bullet}))
.
,
one gets an exact sequence in
{\mathscr{BC}}
, in which
{\mathbb{H}}^{i}(X,{\mathscr{E}}_{j})

denotes the BC whose
C
-points are
H^{i}(X,{\mathscr{E}}_{i})
:

0\to{\mathbb{H}}^{1}(X,{\mathscr{E}}_{-1})\to{\rm BC}({\mathscr{E}}_{-1}\overset{0}{\to}{\mathscr{E}}_{0})\to{\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{E}}_{0})\to 0.

If
{\mathscr{E}}_{-1}\overset{0}{\to}{\mathscr{E}}_{0}

and
{\mathscr{F}}_{-1}\overset{0}{\to}{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
are objects of

{\rm Coh}_{X}^{-}
, then

{\rm Hom}_{\rm BC}({\rm BC}({\mathscr{E}}_{-1}\overset{0}{\to}{\mathscr{E}}_{0}),{\rm BC}({\mathscr{F}}_{-1}\overset{0}{\to}{\mathscr{F}}_{0}))=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm Hom}({\mathscr{E}}_{-1},{\mathscr{F}}_{-1})&{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathscr{E}}_{0},{\mathscr{F}}_{-1})\\
0&{\rm Hom}({\mathscr{E}}_{0},{\mathscr{F}}_{0})\end{pmatrix}

The following theorem is the main result of
[
37
]
.

Theorem 3.12
.

(Le Bras,
[
37
, Th. 1.2]
)

The functor
{\rm BC}
realizes an equivalence of categories

{\rm Coh}^{-}_{X}\simeq{\mathscr{BC}}.

3.2.5.
Harder-Narasimhan filtration on BC’s

One endows
{\rm Coh}_{X}^{-}
with functions rank
{\rm rk}^{-}
, degree
{\rm deg}^{-}

and slope
\mu^{-}=\frac{{\rm deg}^{-}}{{\rm rk}^{-}}
, by setting:

{\rm rk}^{-}({\mathscr{E}}_{-1}\overset{0}{\to}{\mathscr{E}}_{0})=\deg({\mathscr{E}}_{0})-\deg({\mathscr{E}}_{-1}),\quad{\rm deg}^{-}({\mathscr{E}}_{-1}\overset{0}{\to}{\mathscr{E}}_{0})={\rm rk}({\mathscr{E}}_{-1})-{\rm rk}({\mathscr{E}}_{0}),

which turn it into a
Harder-Narasimhan category.

By transport of structure, this endows also
{\mathscr{BC}}
with functions
rank
{\rm rk}^{-}
, degree
{\rm deg}^{-}

and slope
\mu^{-}
that turn it into a Harder-Narasimhan category; we have

{\rm rk}^{-}={\rm dim}\quad{\rm and}\quad\deg^{-}=-{\rm ht}.

If
{\mathscr{F}}_{x}
is torsion, then
\mu^{-}({\rm BC}(0\to{\mathscr{F}}_{x}))=0
.
If
{\mathbb{W}}
is a BC, we let
{\mathbb{W}}^{\geq\lambda},{\mathbb{W}}^{>\lambda}

denote the Harder-Narasimhan filtration, and we set
{\mathbb{W}}^{>-\infty}:=\cup_{\lambda\in{\mathrm{R}}}{\mathbb{W}}^{\geq\lambda}
.

If
\lambda=\frac{d}{h}
(in lowest terms),
denote by
{\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda}
the BC defined by

{\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda}:={\mathbb{U}}_{h,d}
. Note that, if
\lambda=\frac{d}{h}
is in lowest terms and
e\geq 1
, then

{\mathbb{U}}_{eh,ed}={\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda}^{e}
.
Then we have

{\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda}=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)),&{\text{if $\lambda\geq 0$,}}\\
{\mathbb{H}}^{1}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)),&{\text{if $\lambda<0$.}}\end{cases}

Alternatively,

{\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda}=\begin{cases}{\rm BC}(0\to{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)),&{\text{if $\lambda\geq 0$,}}\\
{\rm BC}({\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)\to 0),&{\text{if $\lambda<0$.}}\end{cases}

Then

{\rm rk}^{-}({\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda})={\rm sign}(\lambda)\,d,\quad\deg^{-}({\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda})=-{\rm sign}(\lambda)\,h,\quad\mu^{-}({\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda})=\frac{-1}{\lambda}.

Remark 3.13
.

(i)
Since

{\bf Q}_{p}={\mathbb{U}}_{0}
,
we have

\mu^{-}({\bf Q}_{p})=-\infty
.

(ii) BC’s are naturally diamonds (and were amongst the first non trivial examples of diamonds) and, as such,
have connected components. If
{\mathbb{W}}
is a BC, then
{\mathbb{W}}^{>-\infty}
is the connected component of
0

and the quotient
{\mathbb{W}}^{-\infty}
is the largest étale quotient (group of connected
components, a finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space).

(iii) The Harder-Narasimhan filtration splits (non canonically), and every BC can be decomposed as

(3.14)

{\mathbb{W}}={\mathbb{U}}_{-1/\lambda_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathbb{U}}_{-1/\lambda_{r}}\oplus\big(\oplus_{x}{\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{F}}_{x})\big),

where the
\lambda_{i}
are non zero elements of
{\bf Q}\cup\{-\infty\}
,

{\mathbb{U}}_{-1/\lambda_{i}}
is of slope
\lambda_{i}
, and
{\mathscr{F}}_{x}

is a torsion coherent sheaf, supported at
x
, zero for almost all
x
, and

{\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{F}}_{x})
is of slope
0
.
The
\lambda_{i}
are
the slopes of
{\mathbb{W}}
(to which one has to add
0
if
one of the
{\mathscr{F}}_{x}
is non zero).

(iv) In the exact sequence

0\to{\mathbb{H}}^{1}(X,{\mathscr{E}}_{-1})\to{\rm BC}({\mathscr{E}}_{-1}\overset{0}{\to}{\mathscr{E}}_{0})\to{\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{E}}_{0})\to 0,

the term on the left represents the subspace of slopes
>0
of

{\rm BC}({\mathscr{E}}_{-1}\overset{0}{\to}{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
.

Remark 3.15
.

(i)
The existence of the exact sequence
0\to{\mathbb{W}}^{>-\infty}\to{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{W}}^{-\infty}\to 0
makes it possible to show that a decreasing sequence of BC’s is stationary:
indeed, if
({\mathbb{W}}_{n})_{n\in{\bf N}}
is such a sequence, then
\dim({\mathbb{W}}_{n})
is decreasing and
bounded below by
0
, hence is constant for
n\geq N
. It follows that
{\mathbb{W}}_{N}/{\mathbb{W}}_{n}

is of dimension
0
and hence is a quotient of
{\mathbb{W}}_{N}^{-\infty}
. Since

{\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}}_{N}/{\mathbb{W}}_{n})
is increasing
and bounded by
{\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}}_{N}^{-\infty})<\infty
, one concludes that

{\mathbb{W}}_{N}/{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
is constant for
n
big enough, and that so is
{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
.

(ii) One can also use a presentation to reduce to the case
{\mathbb{W}}={\mathbb{V}}^{d}
, and then
argue by induction on
d
using the fact that a sub-BC of
{\mathbb{V}}^{1}
is either
{\mathbb{V}}^{1}

or a finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space (
[
10
, Prop. 7.13]
ou
[
11
, Prop. 2.1]
).
This proof applies verbatim to almost
C
-representations.

Lemma 3.16
.

(
[
11
, Prop. 2.4]
)

A sub-BC
{\mathbb{W}}
of
{\mathbb{V}}^{N}
, containing no
{\mathbb{V}}^{1}
, satisfies

\dim({\mathbb{W}})<{\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}})
or, equivalently, has slopes
<-1
.

Proof.

We will use the equivalence
{\rm Coh}^{-}_{X}\simeq{\mathscr{BC}}
.
By decomposing
{\mathbb{W}}
as a direct sum of stable BC’s, it suffices to prove the statement for

{\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda}
, with

\lambda=\frac{d}{h}>0
(here we used the fact that
{\mathbb{W}}
does not contain
{\mathbb{V}}^{1}
). This amounts to showing that, if
f:{\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda}\to{\mathbb{V}}^{N}
is an injective map
then
h>d
. Passing to the category
{\rm Coh}^{-}_{X}
, we see that we need to show that if a map
f^{\flat}:{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)\to\iota_{\infty,*}C^{N}
is injective on global sections then
h>d
.
But this map factors as

\textstyle{{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\flat}}
\textstyle{\iota_{\infty,*}C^{N}}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{X,\infty}\otimes{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\iota_{\infty,*}C)\otimes{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\iota_{\infty,*}C^{h}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Tracing this diagram from the left upper corner first vertically then horizontally we obtain a map
\overline{f}:{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)\to\iota_{\infty,*}C^{h}
,
which is injective on global sections. Now, passing back (from the category
{\rm Coh}^{-}_{X}
) to the category
\mathscr{BC}
we get an injective map

\widetilde{f}:{\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda}\to{\mathbb{V}}^{h}
.
Since
{\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda}
is of Dimension
(d,h)
and
{\mathbb{V}}^{h}
of Dimension
(h,0)
,
the existence of an injection implies
h<d
, as wanted.
∎

3.2.6.
Morphisms

We can describe

{\rm Hom}
and
{\rm Ext}^{1}
in the category
\mathscr{BC}
using the curve.
For example:

(1)

If
D_{\lambda}

is the division algebra with center
{\bf Q}_{p}
and invariant
\lambda
,
we have

{\rm End}_{\mathscr{B}C}({\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda})={\rm End}({\mathscr{O}}(\lambda))=D_{\lambda}.

(2)

Recall that, if
\lambda_{1}=\frac{d_{1}}{h_{1}}
,
\lambda_{2}=\frac{d_{2}}{h_{2}}
, and

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=\frac{d}{h}
in minimal form,
we have
{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda_{1})\otimes{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda_{2})={\mathscr{O}}(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})^{n(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}
,
where
n(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\frac{h_{1}}{h_{2}}{h}
. Also:

{\rm Hom}({\mathscr{O}}(\lambda_{1}),{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda_{2}))={\rm Hom}({\mathscr{O}},{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})),\quad{\rm Hom}({\mathscr{O}},{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda))=H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{O}}(\lambda)).

(3)

If
\lambda=\frac{d}{h}\geq 0
then
{\rm Hom}({\mathbb{U}}_{\lambda},{\mathbb{V}}^{1})
is the
C
-module of rank
h

generated by
\theta\circ\varphi^{i}
, for
0\leq i\leq h-1
.

3.2.7.
Slope
0
and curvature
0

Let
x
be a closed point of
X
.
The map
{\mathscr{F}}\mapsto{H}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{F}})
induces an equivalence of categories
from the category of torsion coherent sheaves, supported
at
x
, to the category of finite length
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}(C_{x})
-modules.
A finite length
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}(C_{x})
-module is a direct sum of
{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(C_{x})={\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}(C_{x})/t_{x}^{m}
, and the sheaf
i_{x,*}{\mathbb{B}}_{m}
corresponding to
{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(C_{x})

(where
i_{x}
denotes the inclusion of
x
in
X
) lives in an exact sequence

0\to{\mathscr{O}}\overset{t_{x}^{m}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathscr{O}}(m)\to i_{x,*}{\mathbb{B}}_{m}\to 0

Passing to the sequence of
H^{0}
’s (which is exact as
H^{1}(X,{\mathscr{O}})=0
), this gives an isomorphism

{\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,i_{x,*}{\mathbb{B}}_{m})={\mathbb{U}}_{m}/{\bf Q}_{p}t_{x}^{m}.

One deduces from the equivalence
{\rm Coh}_{X}^{-}\simeq\mathscr{BC}
that

{\rm End}_{\mathscr{BC}}({\mathbb{U}}_{m}/{\bf Q}_{p}t_{x}^{m})\simeq{\rm End}_{{\rm Coh}_{X}}(i_{x,*}{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(C_{x}))\simeq{\rm End}_{{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}(C_{x})}({\mathbb{B}}_{m}(C_{x}))\simeq{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(C_{x}).

In particular, for
m=1
, one obtains

{\rm End}_{\mathscr{BC}}({\mathbb{U}}_{1}/{\bf Q}_{p}t_{x})\simeq C_{x}.

Note also that, if
x\neq\infty
, then

{\rm Hom}_{\mathscr{BC}}({\mathbb{U}}_{1}/{\bf Q}_{p}t_{x},{\mathbb{V}}^{1})\simeq{\rm Hom}_{{\rm Coh}_{X}}(i_{x,*}{\mathbb{B}}_{m},i_{\infty,*}{\mathbb{B}}_{m})=0,

since the two sheaves are supported at distinct points.

In the special case
x=\infty
, which will be crucial for our results,

t_{x}=t
and
{\mathbb{U}}_{m}/{\bf Q}_{p}t^{m}={\mathbb{B}}_{m}
, and
one can describe
directly the object
{\mathbb{M}}
of
\mathscr{BC}
attached to a
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module of finite length
M
:
we have

{\mathbb{M}}=M\otimes_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}.

This can be summarized by the following result.

Proposition 3.17
.

The functor

M\mapsto{\mathbb{M}}=M\otimes_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
defines an equivalence of categories between the category
of
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-modules of finite length and the subcategory of
\mathscr{BC}
of objects of
curvature
0
.

Corollary 3.18
.

(i)

The kernel and cokernel of a morphism of objects of curvature
0
are of curvature
0
.

(ii)
If
{\mathbb{W}}
is a torsion
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module, i.e.,
a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module annihilated by
t^{r}
for some
r\geq 1
,
then
{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})=0
and
{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})=0
.

Proof.

Point (i) is a direct consequence of Proposition
3.17
. To prove point (ii),
we can use Proposition
3.17
, Section
3.2.7
, and the decomposition (
3.14
) to write

{\mathbb{W}}
as
W\otimes_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
for some torsion
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module
W
.
Now we have, using Proposition
3.17
and the fact that
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}=\operatorname{lim}_{k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}
,

{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})=\operatorname{lim}_{k}{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})=\operatorname{lim}_{k}{\rm Hom}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}({W},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})={\rm Hom}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}({W},{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})=0

This proves the result for
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
.

Now, by
[
10
, § 7.3, Lemme 7.10]
, a BC of dimension
1
is a quotient
of
{\bf Q}_{p}^{r}\oplus{\mathbb{L}}_{\ell}
where
{\mathbb{L}}_{\ell}
is the Graph of an additive element
\ell
.
It follows that a BC
{\mathbb{W}}
of dimension
d
is a quotient of

{\bf Q}_{p}^{r}\oplus{\mathbb{L}}_{\ell_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathbb{L}}_{\ell_{d}}
(one can also use universal covers
of
p
-divisible groups instead of Graphs of additive elements
[
37
]
),
and that the image of

\alpha:{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}
is generated by the images
8
8

8

That is, the
image of
{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)
is generated by
\alpha({\bf Q}_{p}^{r})
and
\tau(\alpha(\ell_{i}))-\alpha(\ell_{i})

for
1\leq i\leq d
and
\tau\in\widetilde{T}_{\Lambda}
in the notations of
[
10
, § 7.3]
.

of
{\bf Q}_{p}^{r}
and
\ell_{1},\dots\ell_{d}
,
hence factors through
t^{-N}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
for some
N
.
This makes it possible to use the case of
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
to finish the proof.
∎

3.2.8.
Canonical and Harder-Narasimhan filtrations

Let
{\mathbb{W}}
be a BC.
The relation between the decomposition (
3.14
) and the filtration of Proposition
3.7
is given by:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}

\displaystyle\simeq\big(\oplus_{\lambda_{i}>0}{\mathbb{U}}_{-1/\lambda_{i}}\big)\oplus\big(\oplus_{x\neq\infty}{\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{F}}_{x})\big),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{W}}_{\leq 0}

\displaystyle\simeq\big(\oplus_{\lambda_{i}<0}{\mathbb{U}}_{-1/\lambda_{i}}\big)\oplus{\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{F}}_{\infty})={\mathbb{H}}^{0}\big(X,{\mathscr{F}}_{\infty}\oplus\big(\oplus_{\lambda_{i}<0}{\mathscr{O}}(-1/\lambda_{i})\big)\big),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{W}}_{<0}

\displaystyle\simeq\oplus_{\lambda_{i}<0}{\mathbb{U}}_{-1/\lambda_{i}},\quad{\mathbb{W}}_{=0}\simeq{\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{F}}_{\infty}).

In particular,
{\mathbb{W}}
is of curvature
<0
if and only if its Harder-Narasimhan slopes
are
<0
, and if its slopes are
>0
then
{\mathbb{W}}
is of curvature
>0
.

From the properties of Harder-Narasimhan filtrations and the above decompositions,
we can deduce the following results.

Corollary 3.19
.

(i)

{\mathbb{W}}
