is of curvature
<0

(
resp.
\leq 0
)
if and only if
{\mathbb{W}}\simeq H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{E}})
,
where
{\mathscr{E}}
is a vector bundle of slopes
\geq 0

(
resp. the sum of a vector
bundle of slopes
\geq 0
and a torsion sheaf supported at
\infty
)
.

(ii)
An extension of two BC’s of curvature
<0

(
resp.
\leq 0
)
is
of curvature
<0

(
resp.
\leq 0
)
.

Corollary 3.20
.

The sign of the curvature determines the sign of the height
:

(a)

curvature
0
implies height
0
;

(b)
curvature
<0
implies height
>0
;

(c)
curvature
>0
implies
9
9

9

If
x\neq\infty
, then
{\rm ht}({\mathbb{U}}_{1}/{\bf Q}_{p}t_{x})=0
,
but the curvature of
{\mathbb{U}}_{1}/{\bf Q}_{p}t_{x}
is
>0
, which shows that we can’t replace
“height
\leq 0
” by “height
<0
”.
height
\leq 0
.

Corollary 3.21
.

The curvature decreases by going to a subobject and increases by taking a quotient:

(i)
A sub-BC of a BC of curvature
\leq 0

(
resp.
<0
)
has curvature
\leq 0

(
resp.
<0
)
.

(ii)

A sub-BC of height
0
of a BC of curvature
\leq 0
has curvature
0
.

(iii)

A quotient of a BC of curvature
\geq 0

(
resp.
>0
)
has curvature
\geq 0

(
resp.
>0
)
.

(iv)

A quotient of height
0
of a BC of curvature
\geq 0
has curvature
0
.

Remark 3.22
.

An important consequence of (ii) of Corollary
3.21
is that a sub-BC
{\mathbb{U}}
of
a torsion
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module
{\mathbb{W}}
satisfies
{\rm ht}({\mathbb{U}})\geq 0
and

{\mathbb{U}}
is itself a torsion
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module if and only if
{\rm ht}({\mathbb{U}})=0
.

This can be proven, without the Harder-Narasimhan decomposition, by induction on the length
of
{\mathbb{W}}
, using the fact that a sub-BC of
{\mathbb{V}}^{1}
is either
{\mathbb{V}}^{1}

or a finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space and the fact that an extension
of
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Modules is itself a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module. This proof extends verbatim
to almost
C
-representations thanks to Proposition
2.5
.

Proposition 3.23
.

Let
f:{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
be a morphism of BC’s with
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module.
Assume that
{\rm Im}(f)
generates
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
as a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module. Then, if
{\rm Coker}(f)\neq 0
,
it is of curvature
>0
and height
<0
.

Proof.

We can assume
f
to be injective (mod out
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
by
{\rm Ker}(f)
if this is not the case),
and not surjective (otherwise there is nothing to prove).
Then
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}={\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{F}}_{1})
and
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}={\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{F}}_{2})
where
{\mathscr{F}}_{1},{\mathscr{F}}_{2}

are coherent sheaves on
X_{\rm FF}
with vanishing
H^{1}
,

{\mathscr{F}}_{2}
is supported at
\infty
,
and
f
is induced from a morphism
f_{X}:{\mathscr{F}}_{1}\to{\mathscr{F}}_{2}
of coherent sheaves.
The hypothesis that
{\rm Im}(f)
generates
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
implies that
f_{X}
is
surjective and the injectivity of
f
implies that
{\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\rm Ker}(f_{X}))=0
. Vanishing of

{\mathbb{H}}^{1}(X,{\mathscr{F}}_{1})
gives an isomorphism
{\rm Coker}(f)\simeq{\mathbb{H}}^{1}(X,{\rm Ker}(f_{X}))
.
Now,
{\mathscr{F}}_{1}
is not torsion (otherwise it would be supported at
\infty
since
its
{\mathbb{H}}^{0}
has curvature
\leq 0
, and
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
would be a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module, and
f
would be
surjective which we assumed to not be the case); hence
{\rm Ker}(f_{X})
is not torsion.
Since its
H^{0}
is
0
, its Harder-Narasimhan slopes are
<0
,
which implies that
{\rm Coker}(f)
is of curvature
>0
and height
<0
, as desired.
∎

3.2.9.
BC’s of curvature
\leq 0

Lemma 3.24
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

{\mathbb{W}}
is of curvature
\leq 0
.

(ii)
There is an exact sequence

(3.25)

0\to V\to{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{M}}\to 0,

where
{\mathbb{M}}
is of curvature
0

and
V
is finite dimensional over
{\bf Q}_{p}
.

Proof.

Implication
(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
follows from the fact that, if
{\mathbb{W}}
is of curvature
\leq 0
, then

{\mathbb{W}}={\mathbb{H}}^{0}(X,{\mathscr{F}}_{\infty})\oplus\big(\oplus_{d_{i}/h_{i}\geq 0}{\mathbb{U}}_{d_{i}/h_{i}}\big)

and we have an exact sequence
0\to{\bf Q}_{p^{h_{i}}}\to{\mathbb{U}}_{d_{i}/h_{i}}\to{\mathbb{B}}_{d_{i}}\to 0
.
Then
V=\oplus_{d_{i}/h_{i}}{\bf Q}_{p^{h_{i}}}
gives what we want.

The converse implication (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) follows from point (ii) of Corollary
3.19
.
∎

3.3.
Categorification of height

We will introduce now a partial categorification of height of BC’s.
If
{\mathbb{W}}
is a BC, set

h({\mathbb{W}}):={\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})

This is a
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
-module; hence
h(-)
is a functor
from the category
\mathscr{BC}
to the category of vector spaces over
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
.
Thanks to the arguments in the proof of Corollary
3.18
,
we also have

h({\mathbb{W}})={\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{k\geq 0}{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},t^{-k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})={\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})\otimes_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}

We recall (see
[
2
]
) that we also have

{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{k\geq 0}{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},t^{-k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}).

This is a
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
-module as well.

Lemma 3.26
.

(i)
If
{\mathbb{W}}
is of curvature
\leq 0
,
then
{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})=0
.

(ii)

If
0\to{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}\to 0
is an exact sequence of BC’s
of curvature
\leq 0
,
the sequence
0\to h({\mathbb{W}}_{2})\to h({\mathbb{W}})\to h({\mathbb{W}}_{1})\to 0
is exact.

Proof.

Claim (ii) follows immediately from (i).

Now,
the exact sequence (
3.25
) shows that it suffices to prove (i)
for an affine and for
{\bf Q}_{p}
.
That
{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\bf Q}_{p},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})=0
follows easily, by devissage, from the fact that the maps
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{m+1}\to{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{m}
are surjective and

{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\bf Q}_{p},{\mathbb{V}}^{1})=0
(see
[
37
, Th. 4.1]
). This implies that

{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\bf Q}_{p},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{k\geq 0}{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\bf Q}_{p},t^{-k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})=0,

as wanted.

To show that
{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})=0
for
{\mathbb{W}}
affine, it suffices,
again by devissage, to show that
{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{V}}^{1},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})=0
.
To show the latter fact we use
the exact sequence

0\to t^{-k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\to t^{-k-1}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\to t^{-k-1}{\mathbb{V}}^{1}\to 0

and the fact that
{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{V}}^{1},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})=0
. This gives us the exact sequence

0\to{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{V}}^{1},t^{-k-1}{\mathbb{V}}^{1})\to{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{V}}^{1},t^{-k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})\to{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{V}}^{1},t^{-k-1}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}).

Since the first two terms are isomorphic to

{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t
as
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-modules,

{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{V}}^{1},t^{-k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})\to{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{V}}^{1},t^{-k-1}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})
is zero.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.27
.

(i)
If
{\mathbb{W}}
is of curvature
\leq 0
then

{\rm rk}(h({\mathbb{W}}))={\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}})
.

(ii)

In general,
{\rm rk}(h({\mathbb{W}}))={\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}})+{\rm rk}({\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}))
.

Proof.

For (i),
by Lemma
3.24
, we have the exact sequence

0\to V\to{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{M}}\to 0
, where
{\mathbb{M}}
is affine.
This yields the exact sequence

0\to h({\mathbb{M}})\to h({\mathbb{W}})\to h(V)\to{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{M}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}).

Since
h({\mathbb{M}})=0
and we have
{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{M}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})=0
, by Lemma
3.26
, this sequence implies that

{\rm rk}(h({\mathbb{W}}))=\dim_{{\bf Q}_{p}}V
. We are done because

{\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}})=\dim_{{\bf Q}_{p}}V
.

(ii) Follows via the exact sequence
of
{\rm Ext}
of a presentation:
if
0\to V\to{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{W}}\to 0
represents
{\mathbb{W}}
, where
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}
is an extension
of
{\mathbb{V}}^{d}
by a
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space
V^{\prime}
of finite dimension, arguing as for (1) we get the following diagram with exact row:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h({\mathbb{W}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h({\mathbb{W}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{h(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}}^{\prime},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{h(V^{\prime})}

Since
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}
is of curvature
\leq 0
, by Lemma
3.26
, we have
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}}^{\prime},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})=0
. It follows that

{\rm rk}(h({\mathbb{W}}))={\rm rk}(h(V^{\prime}))-{\rm rk}(h(V))+{\rm rk}(\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})),

which gives us what we wanted because
{\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}})={\rm rk}(h(V^{\prime}))-{\rm rk}(h(V))
.
∎

4.
The category
q{\mathscr{BC}}

We need to enlarge the category
\mathscr{BC}
to allow extensions by arbitrary
{\mathbb{B}}_{m}
-Modules,
for
m\geq 1
.

4.1.
Categories of Topological Vector Spaces

We start by putting topology on Vector Spaces.

4.1.1.
Topological Vector Spaces and
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pairs

Definition 4.1
.

A
Topological Vector Space

{\mathbb{W}}

is a functor
\Lambda\mapsto{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)

from sympathetic algebras to
{\rm LH}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})

with the property that the image of
{\rm Hom}(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})\times B

is bounded in
{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda^{\prime})
for
B
bounded in
{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)
.

\bullet

A morphism
{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}
of Topological Vector Spaces, is a natural transformation
of functors.

\bullet
A sequence

0\to{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}\to 0
of Topological Vector Spaces is exact if and only if

0\to{\mathbb{W}}_{1}(\Lambda)\to{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}(\Lambda)\to 0

is exact in
{\rm LH}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})
, for every
\Lambda
.

Remark 4.2
.

The condition on the image of
{\rm Hom}(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})\times B
is automatic
if
{\mathbb{W}}
is a BC. This follows form the decomposition (
3.14
) or the existence of a covering
of
{\mathbb{W}}
by a direct sum of universal covers of
p
-divisible groups (these universal covers
have natural lattices which are functorial in
\Lambda
).

Remark 4.3
.

Following
[
44
, Cor. 1.2.20]
, we will represent the category
{\rm LH}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})
as the localization of the full subcategory of the homotopy category of
C_{{\bf Q}_{p}}
consisting of complexes
E
of the form

0\to E_{1}\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\delta_{E}}}E_{0}\to 0,

where
E_{0}
is in degree
0
and
\delta_{E}
is a monomorphism, by the multiplicative system formed by morphisms
u:E\to F
such that the square

\textstyle{F_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{F}}
\textstyle{F_{0}}
\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{E}}
\scriptstyle{u_{1}}
\textstyle{E_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{0}}

is bicartesian. Note that this implies that for
E\in{\rm LH}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})
the (algebraic) quotient
E_{0}/E_{1}
is well-defined.

We denote by
{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}(-,-)
and
{\rm Ext}^{i}_{\rm TVS}(-,-)

the
{\rm{Hom}}
and Yoneda
\operatorname{Ext}
groups in the
category of Topological Vector Space.

Definition 4.4
.

(i) A
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module

{\mathbb{W}}
is a VS such that there exists a topological
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module
W
(i.e., a
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module in the category
C_{{\bf Q}_{p}}
)
killed by a power of
t
(hence
W
is a
{\bf B}_{m}
-module for
m
big enough) such that

{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)={\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}(\Lambda)\widehat{\otimes}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}W
for all
\Lambda
(if
W
is killed by
t^{m}
,
we also have
10
10

10

{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)\widehat{\otimes}_{{\bf B}_{m}}W
is defined as the quotient of

{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)\widehat{\otimes}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}W
by the closure of the subspace generated by the

\lambda a\otimes w-a\otimes\lambda w
, for
a\in{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)
,
\lambda\in{\bf B}_{m}

and
w\in W
. If we choose a Banach basis
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\Lambda
over
C
(note that

I
is countable by our asumptions on
\Lambda
’s), and a lifting
\hat{e}_{i}
of

e_{i}
in
{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)
, then
(\hat{e}_{i})_{i\in I}
is a Banach basis
of
{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)
over
{\bf B}_{m}
, and we get isomorphisms
{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)\simeq\ell^{\infty}_{0}(I,{\bf B}_{m})
(bounded sequences tending to
0
at
\infty
), and

{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)\widehat{\otimes}_{{\bf B}_{m}}W\simeq\ell^{\infty}_{0}(I,{\bf B}_{m})\widehat{\otimes}_{{\bf B}_{m}}W\simeq\ell^{\infty}_{0}(I,W)
of
{\bf B}_{m}
-modules
(to recover the structure of
{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)
-module, one needs to express multiplication
by
\lambda\in{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)
in the basis
(\hat{e}_{i})_{i\in I}
).

{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)={\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)\widehat{\otimes}_{{\bf B}_{m}}W
for all
\Lambda
).
In particular
{\mathbb{W}}(C)=W
.
A morphism
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
of
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Modules is a morphism of TVS’s such that

{\mathbb{W}}_{1}(\Lambda)\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}(\Lambda)
is
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}(\Lambda)
-linear for every
\Lambda
.

(ii) A
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pair
is a Topological Vector Space
({\mathbb{W}}_{1},{\mathbb{W}}_{2})
obtained from a pair
(W_{1},W_{2})
of topological
{\bf B}_{m}
-modules, for some
m

(i.e., on
C
-points, we have an injection
W_{1}\to W_{2}
of topological
{\bf B}_{m}
-modules),
by base change as above. Note that
{\mathbb{W}}_{1},{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
are
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Modules. A morphism of
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pairs is a morphism of TVSs that is
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-linear.

We denote by
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(-,-)
and
{\rm Ext}^{i}_{{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(-,-)
the
{\rm{Hom}}
and
\operatorname{Ext}
groups in the
category of
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pairs.
We have natural maps

{\rm Hom}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(W,W^{\prime})\to{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime})\to{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime})

and the same for
\operatorname{Ext}
groups.
It follows from Theorem
4.30
below that, if
W,W^{\prime}
are reasonable of the same type, these maps are isomorphisms.

4.1.2.
qBC’s

Definition 4.5
.

(i) A
qBC
is a Topological Vector Space
{\mathbb{W}}
admitting a filtration

0\subset{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}\subset{\mathbb{W}}_{\geq 0}\subset{\mathbb{W}}

with:

\bullet

{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}
– a BC of curvature
>0
,

\bullet

{\mathbb{W}}_{=0}:={\mathbb{W}}_{\geq 0}/{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}
–
a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pair,

\bullet

{\mathbb{W}}_{<0}:={\mathbb{W}}/{\mathbb{W}}_{\geq 0}
–
a BC of curvature
<0
.

(ii) A
morphism
{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}
of qBC’s
is a morphism of TVS’s compatible with the
filtrations and such that the induced map
{\mathbb{W}}_{=0}\to{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}_{=0}
is a morphism
of
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pairs.

(iii) The
height
{\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}})
of a qBC

{\mathbb{W}}
is defined as:

{\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}}):={\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}}_{>0})+{\rm ht}({\mathbb{W}}_{<0})

It only depends on the isomorphism class of
{\mathbb{W}}
.

Remark 4.6
.

Assume that
{\mathbb{W}}_{=0}
is a reasonable
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pair
(see Section
4.3
for the definition).

(i) As shown in Proposition
4.26
,

{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}
is the largest sub-BC of
{\mathbb{W}}
of curvature
>0
,
and
{\mathbb{W}}_{<0}
is the largest
quotient-BC of
{\mathbb{W}}
of curvature
<0
.
Hence the filtration on
{\mathbb{W}}
is uniquely
determined.

(ii) If
{\mathbb{W}}
and
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}
are qBC’s, and

{\mathbb{W}}_{=0}
and
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}_{=0}
are reasonable
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pairs of the same type,
we show (see Corollary
4.31
)
that any morphism
{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}
of TVS’s is a morphism of qBC’s
(the most difficult part of the proof is to show that the induced morphism

{\mathbb{W}}_{=0}\to{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}_{=0}
is a morphism
de
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pairs).

Definition 4.7
.

(
Curvature
)
Let
{\mathbb{W}}\in q{\mathscr{BC}}
. We say that

{\mathbb{W}}
has:

\bullet
curvature
>0
, if
{\mathbb{W}}={\mathbb{W}}_{>0}
.

\bullet
curvature
\geq 0
, if
{\mathbb{W}}={\mathbb{W}}_{\geq 0}
.

\bullet
curvature
=0
, if
{\mathbb{W}}={\mathbb{W}}_{=0}
(i.e. if
{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}={\mathbb{W}}_{<0}=0
).

\bullet
curvature
<0
, if
{\mathbb{W}}={\mathbb{W}}_{<0}
(i.e. if
{\mathbb{W}}_{\geq 0}=0
).

\bullet
curvature
\leq 0
, if
{\mathbb{W}}={\mathbb{W}}_{\leq 0}
(i.e. if
{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}=0
).

Remark 4.8
.

(i) It is conceivable that the filtration on a qBC splits and that a qBC can be written
(non canonically) as the direct sum of a BC and a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pair, but proving such a statement
requires to understand
{\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathbb{Y}},{\mathbb{W}})
where
{\mathbb{Y}}
is a BC and

{\mathbb{W}}
is a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module. Presumably
11
11

11

As we will show in a sequel to this paper,
this vanishing always holds but does not imply that any qBC can be written
as the direct sum of a BC and a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pair, only that a qBC can be written as
an extension of a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pair by a BC. On the other hand, it allows to prove that

q{\mathscr{BC}}
is an abelian category.
, this group is
0
(this is the case if
{\mathbb{W}}

is of finite length), but this remains to be proved (and point (ii) below shows
that strange things can happen passing from
C_{{\bf Q}_{p}}
to
{\rm LH}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})
).

(ii)
If
W\in C_{K}
is Hausdorff and
V
is finite dimensional,
then any strict exact sequence
0\to V\to W^{\prime}\to W\to 0
splits (
[
14
, Lemma 2.5]
).
This is not always
the case if
W\in{\rm LH}(C_{K})
; indeed, if
W=(W_{1}\to W_{2})
with
W_{1},W_{2}
Hausdorff, then we have
an exact sequence

0\to{\rm Hom}(W,V)\to{\rm Hom}(W_{2},V)\to{\rm Hom}(W_{1},V)\to{\rm Ext}^{1}(W,V)\to 0

(The next term
{\rm Ext}^{1}(W_{2},V)
vanishes since
W_{2}
is Hausdorff.)

If
W_{1}
is dense in
W_{2}
, then
{\rm Hom}(W,V)=0
and

{\rm Ext}^{1}(W,V)={\rm Hom}(W_{1},V)/{\rm Hom}(W_{2},V)
.
For example, if
K={\bf Q}_{p}
,
W_{1}={\rm LA}({\bf Z}_{p},{\bf Q}_{p})
,
W_{2}={\mathscr{C}}({\bf Z}_{p},{\bf Q}_{p})
, and
V={\bf Q}_{p}
,
then
{\rm Ext}^{1}(W,V)={\mathscr{D}}({\bf Z}_{p},{\bf Q}_{p})/{\rm Mes}({\bf Z}_{p},{\bf Q}_{p})
which is a big space
in which
0
is dense (it is better to view it as an element of
{\rm LH}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})
).

4.2.
A perfectoid point of view on
{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{V}}^{1},{\mathbb{V}}^{1})

If
\Lambda
is a sympathetic algebra, we denote by
\overline{\Lambda\{X\}}
the sympathetic
closure of
\Lambda\{X\}
, by

H_{\Lambda\{X\}}
the group
{\rm Aut}_{\rm cont}(\overline{\Lambda\{X\}}/\Lambda\{X\})

and by
T_{\Lambda}
the extension of
{\mathscr{O}}_{\Lambda}
by
H_{\Lambda\{X\}}
considered
in
[
10
, Prop. 5.23]

(it is the group of continuous automorphisms
\tau
of
\overline{\Lambda\{X\}}
whose
restriction to

\Lambda\{X\}
is of the form
X\mapsto X+x(\tau)
, with
x(\tau)\in{\mathscr{O}}_{\Lambda}
; the map

\tau\mapsto x(\tau)
is a surjective group morphism whose kernel is

H_{\Lambda\{X\}}
). We denote by
0
the neutral element of
T_{\Lambda}
.

We fix a morphism
\alpha_{C}:\overline{C\{X\}}\to C
with
\alpha_{C}(X)=0
, and we define

f(\tau)
, if
\tau\in T_{C}
and
f\in\overline{C\{X\}}
, by
f(\tau):=\alpha_{C}(\tau(f))
;
in particular,
X(\tau)=x(\tau)
and
f(0)=\alpha_{C}(f)
.

If
\alpha\in C
is such that
p^{n}\alpha\in 2p{\mathscr{O}}_{C}
, let

E_{\alpha}\in\overline{C\{X\}}
be the unique

p^{n}
-th root of
e^{p^{n}\alpha\,X}
with
E_{\alpha}(0)=1
.
There exists a character
\chi_{\alpha}:T_{C}\to C^{\boldsymbol{*}}
such that

\tau(E_{\alpha})=\chi_{\alpha}(\tau)E_{\alpha}
and an additive character

\psi_{\alpha}:H_{C\{X\}}\to{\bf Q}_{p}
such that
\chi_{\alpha}(\sigma)^{\flat}=\varepsilon^{\psi_{\alpha}(\sigma)}

(note that
\chi_{\alpha}
takes values in
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{p^{\infty}}
on
H_{C\{X\}}
).
Since
\chi_{\alpha}
is a character,
\psi_{\alpha}(\tau^{-1}\sigma\tau)=\psi_{\alpha}(\sigma)

if
\sigma\in H_{C\{X\}}
and
\tau\in T_{C}
.

Lemma 4.9
.

{\mathscr{O}}_{C}\{X\}\big[\frac{E_{1/p^{n}}-1}{p^{1/p^{n+1}}},\ n\in{\bf N}\big]
is integral over
{\mathscr{O}}_{C\{X\}}

and perfectoid
12
12

12

I.e., there exists
r>0
such that
x\mapsto x^{p}
is surjective modulo
p^{r}
.
.

Proof.

Set
Y_{n}=\frac{E_{1/p^{n}}-1}{p^{1/p^{n+1}}}
. Then
(p^{1/p}Y_{0}+1)^{p}=e^{pX}
, hence

Y_{0}^{p}+p^{(p-1)/p}Y_{0}^{p-1}+\tfrac{p-1}{2}p^{(p-2)/p}Y_{0}^{p-2}+\cdots+p^{1/p}Y_{0}=\tfrac{1}{p}(e^{px}-1)

which proves that
Y_{0}
is integral over
{\mathscr{O}}_{C}\{X\}
and that
Y_{0}^{p}=X
modulo
p^{1/p}
.

The same computations, strating from

(p^{1/p^{n+1}}Y_{n}+1)^{p}=p^{1/p^{n}}Y_{n-1}+1
, show that
Y_{n}
is integral over
{\mathscr{O}}_{C}[Y_{n-1}]

and that
Y_{n}^{p}=Y_{n-1}
modulo
p^{1/p}
.

The result follows.
∎

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
0\to{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}\to 0
be an exact sequence of TVS’s
with fixed isomorphims
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\cong{\mathbb{V}}^{1}
and
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}\cong{\mathbb{V}}^{1}
.
Let
v\in C
and

h\in{\mathbb{E}}(\overline{C\{X\}})
lifting
vX\in{\mathbb{V}}^{1}(\overline{C\{X\}})
.

(i)
There exists
\lambda_{v}\in C
depending only on
v
,
and
g\in{\mathbb{W}}_{1}(\overline{C\{X\}})
with
\|g\|\leq p\sup_{\tau\in T_{C}}\|\tau(h)-h-h(\tau)\|
,
such that, if
\ell=h-g
,
we have
(\sigma-1)\ell=\lambda_{v}\psi_{1}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in H_{C\{X\}}
.

(ii)
There exists
\lambda_{\mathbb{E}}\in C
such that

\lambda_{v}=v\lambda_{\mathbb{E}}
for all
v\in C
,
and
{\mathbb{E}}\mapsto\lambda_{\mathbb{E}}
induces
an isomorphism
{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{V}}^{1},{\mathbb{V}}^{1})\simeq C
.

Proof.

\sigma\mapsto h_{\sigma}:=(\sigma-1)h
is a continuous
1
-cocycle on
H_{C\{X\}}

with values in
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}(\overline{C\{X\}})=\overline{C\{X\}}
.
The standard “almost étale descent” and “decompletion” arguments give

g\in{\mathbb{W}}_{1}(\overline{C\{X\}})
with
\|g\|\leq p\sup_{\tau\in T_{C}}\|h_{\sigma}\|
, and
\lambda_{h}\in C\{X\}

such that
(\sigma-1)h=\lambda_{h}\psi_{1}(\sigma)+(\sigma-1)g
, for all
\sigma\in H_{C\{X\}}
;
moreover,
\lambda_{h}
is uniquely determined (in other words,
H^{1}(H_{C\{X\}},\overline{C\{X\}})\simeq C\{X\}
; the inverse isomorphism being
\lambda\mapsto\lambda\psi_{1}
).
If
h^{\prime}
is another lifting of
vX
, then
\sigma\mapsto h^{\prime}_{\sigma}:=(\sigma-1)h^{\prime}

differs from
\sigma\mapsto h_{\sigma}
by a coboundary, hence
\lambda_{h}=\lambda_{h^{\prime}}
;
it follows that we can set
\lambda_{v}=\lambda_{h}
for any choice of
h
.

Now, if
\tau\in T_{C}
, then
\tau\cdot h-h(\tau)
is another lifting of
vX
; hence

\sigma\mapsto(\sigma-1)(\tau\cdot h-h-h(\tau))
is a coboundary.
We also have
(\sigma-1)(\tau\cdot h-h-h(\tau))=\tau(\tau^{-1}\sigma\tau-1)h-(\sigma-1)h
; we deduce that

\tau\cdot(\lambda_{h}\psi_{1}(\tau^{-1}\sigma\tau))=\lambda_{h}\psi_{1}(\sigma)
, and since

\psi_{1}(\tau^{-1}\sigma\tau)=\psi_{1}(\sigma)
, this implies that
\lambda_{h}
is invariant by
translation by
{\mathscr{O}}_{C}
, hence
\lambda_{h}\in C
.

This proves (i). To prove (ii), we have to prove surjectivity and
injectivity. Surjectivity follows from the
fact that
{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{V}}^{1},{\mathbb{V}}^{1})
is a module over
{\rm End}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{V}}^{1})=C

and the fact that
\lambda_{v}\neq 0
if
{\mathbb{E}}={\mathbb{B}}_{2}
and
v\neq 0
,
the injection
{\mathbb{V}}^{1}\to{\mathbb{B}}_{2}
being
x\mapsto tx
.
In fact, one checks that

\lambda_{\mathbb{E}}=1

for
{\mathbb{E}}={\mathbb{B}}_{2}
by using

\ell:=\hat{v}\log[X_{1}^{\flat}]\in{\mathbb{B}}_{2}(\overline{C\{X\}})

for a lifting
of
vX\in{\mathbb{V}}^{1}(C\{X\})
, where
X_{1}^{\flat}=(X_{1},X_{1/p},X_{1/p^{2}},\dots)
and
\hat{v}\in{\bf B}_{2}

is a lifting of
v
: one has
(\sigma-1)\ell=v\psi_{1}(\sigma)
, if
\sigma\in H_{C\{X\}}
,
by definition of
\psi_{1}
.

To prove injectivity, it is enough to check that, if
v\neq 0
and
\lambda_{v}=0
, then

{\mathbb{E}}
is split;
this requires some preparation and is proven in Remark
4.14
. Once injectivity
is proven, it follows that any nontrivial
{\mathbb{E}}
is isomorphic to

{\mathbb{B}}_{2}
, and the existence of
\lambda_{\mathbb{E}}
follows from its existence for
{\mathbb{B}}_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

For all
\Lambda
:

(i)
We have

\bullet

c_{\tau}:=(\tau-1)\ell-\alpha_{\Lambda}(\tau(\ell))\in\Lambda\{X\}\subset{\mathbb{W}}_{1}(\overline{\Lambda\{X\}})
for all
\tau\in T_{\Lambda}
;

\bullet

c_{\tau}=c_{\sigma\tau}=c_{\tau\sigma}

for all
\tau\in T_{\Lambda}
and
\sigma\in H_{\Lambda\{X\}}
.

(ii)
There exists
u\in C\{X\}\subset{\mathbb{W}}_{1}(\overline{C\{X\}})
such that

\ell_{v}:=\ell-u
satisfies
\alpha_{\Lambda}(\tau_{1}\tau_{2}(\ell_{v})-\tau_{1}(\ell_{v})-\tau_{2}(\ell_{v})+\ell_{v})=0

for all
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in T_{\Lambda}

(i.e.
\ell_{v}
is
an additive lifting of
vX
)
.

Proof.

If
\sigma\in H_{\Lambda\{X\}}
, we have
(\sigma-1)c_{\tau}=(\sigma-1)(\tau-1)\ell=\tau(\tau^{-1}\sigma\tau-1)\ell-(\sigma-1)\ell=\lambda_{v}(\psi_{1}(\tau^{-1}\sigma\tau)-\psi_{1}(\sigma))=0
,
which proves the first statement.

Now
c_{\tau\sigma}-c_{\tau}=\tau((\sigma-1)\ell)-\alpha_{\Lambda}(\tau\sigma(\ell))+\alpha_{\Lambda}(\tau(\ell))
. But
\sigma(\ell)=\ell+\alpha_{C}(\sigma(\ell))
;
hence
\alpha_{\Lambda}(\tau\sigma(\ell))-\alpha_{\Lambda}(\tau(\ell))=\alpha_{C}(\sigma(\ell))

and
\tau((\sigma-1)\ell)=\alpha_{C}(\sigma(\ell))
, which proves
that
c_{\tau\sigma}=c_{\tau}
.
If one applies this to
\tau^{-1}\sigma\tau\in H_{\Lambda\{X\}}
instead of
\sigma
,
one gets
c_{\sigma\tau}=c_{\tau}
which proves (i).

Now (i) allows to consider
\tau\mapsto c_{\tau}
as a function on
{\mathscr{O}}_{\Lambda}

with values in
\Lambda\{X\}
; let us denote
F(X,x(\tau))
the function corresponding
to
c_{\tau}
.
We let
F(X,Y)\in C\{X,Y\}
be the function corresponding to
c_{\tau}
,
with
\tau\in T_{C\{Y\}}
satisfying
x(\tau)=Y
.
Then if
s_{y}:\overline{C\{X,Y\}}\to\overline{\Lambda\{X\}}
sends
Y
to
y\in{\mathscr{O}}_{\Lambda}

and
X
to
X
,
we have
F(X,y)=s_{y}(F(X,Y))
: indeed, from
[
10
, Prop. 5.20]
, we infer that,
if
\tau_{y}\in T_{\Lambda}
and
\alpha_{\Lambda}:\overline{\Lambda\{X\}}\to\Lambda

are given, with
x(\tau_{y})=y
, then there exists
unique
\tau_{Y}\in T_{C\{Y\}}
with
x(\tau_{Y})=Y
and
\alpha_{C\{Y\}}:\overline{C\{X,Y\}}\to\overline{C\{Y\}}
making the following diagram commute

\textstyle{\overline{C\{X,Y\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{s_{y}}
\textstyle{\overline{C\{X,Y\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{C\{Y\}}}
\scriptstyle{s_{y}}
\textstyle{\overline{C\{Y\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{y}}
\textstyle{\overline{\Lambda\{X\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{y}}
\textstyle{\overline{\Lambda\{X\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{\Lambda}}
\textstyle{\Lambda}

which gives

s_{y}(F(X,Y))=s_{y}((\tau_{Y}-1)\ell-\alpha_{C\{Y\}}(\tau_{Y}(\ell)))=(\tau_{y}-1)\ell-\alpha_{\Lambda}(\tau_{y}(\ell))=F(X,y)

Now, we have

(4.12)

c_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}-\tau_{1}c_{\tau_{2}}-c_{\tau_{1}}=-\alpha_{\Lambda}(\tau_{1}\tau_{2}(\ell)-\tau_{1}(\ell)-\tau_{2}(\ell))

By construction,
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\mapsto c_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}-\tau_{1}c_{\tau_{2}}-c_{\tau_{1}}

is a
2
-cocycle which we view as a
2
-cocycle
on
{\mathscr{O}}_{\Lambda}
with values in
\Lambda
.
Using the function
F(X,Y)
, this translates into

F(X,Y_{1}+Y_{2})-F(X+Y_{1},Y_{2})-F(X,Y_{1}):=f(Y_{1},Y_{2})\in C\{Y_{1},Y_{2}\}

the
2
-cocycle condition
becoming
f(Y_{2},Y_{3})-f(Y_{1}+Y_{2},Y_{3})+f(Y_{1},Y_{2}+Y_{3})-f(Y_{1},Y_{2})=0
.
Lemma
4.13
gives us
u\in C\{X\}
such that, if

F^{\prime}(X,Y):=F(X,Y)-(u(X+Y)-u(X)-u(Y))

then

F^{\prime}(X,Y_{1}+Y_{2})-F^{\prime}(X+Y_{1},Y_{2})-F^{\prime}(X,Y_{1})=0
.
Setting
\ell_{v}=\ell-u
and
c^{\prime}_{\tau}:=(\tau-1)\ell_{v}-\alpha_{\Lambda}(\tau(\ell_{v}))
,
formula (
4.12
) implies
\alpha_{\Lambda}(\tau_{1}\tau_{2}(\ell_{v})-\tau_{1}(\ell_{v})-\tau_{2}(\ell_{v}))=0
, as wanted.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

If
f\in{\mathscr{O}}_{C}\{Y,Z\}
satisfies
f(Y,Z)-f(X+Y,Z)+f(X,Y+Z)-f(X,Y)=0
,
there exists
u\in p^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{C}\{X\}
such that
f(Y,Z)=u(Y+Z)-u(Y)-u(Z)
.

Proof.

By substracting
f(0,0)
to
f
(and adding
f(0,0)
to
u
), we can assume
f(0,0)=0
.
Now, setting
Y=0
in the above cocycle relation, we get
f(0,Z)-f(X,0)=0
, hence

f(0,Z)=0
and
f(Y,0)=0
. So we can write

f(Y,Z)=Yf_{1}(Z)+Y^{2}f_{2}(Z)+\cdots
Taking derivative with respect to
Y
and putting
Y=0
,
we get
u^{\prime}=f_{1}
if
u
exists,
hence we have to take for
u
the solution in
C[[X]]
with
u(0)=0
.
Substrating
u(Y+Z)-u(Y)-u(Z)
to
f(Y,Z)
, we get
f^{(1)}
still satisfying the
cocycle relation but with a Taylor expansion

Y^{2}f_{2}^{(1)}(Z)+Y^{3}f_{3}^{(1)}(Z)+\cdots
. An easy induction shows that
f_{k}^{(1)}=0

for all
k
(consider the cocycle relation modulo
(Y,Z)^{k+1}
). Hence

f(Y,Z)=u(Y+Z)-u(Y)-u(Z)
.

It remains to check that
u\in p^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{C}\{X\}
. If
u=\sum_{k\geq 1}a_{k}X^{k}
,
and
f(Y,Z)=\sum_{i,j\geq 1}b_{i,j}Y^{i}Z^{j}
, then
a_{k}=\frac{i!}{k(k-1)\cdots(k-i+1)}b_{i,k-i}

for any
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
. By taking
i=1
if
(k,p)=1
, or
i=p^{r}
with
r=v_{p}(k)
if
r\geq 1
,
we obtain that
a_{k}=u_{k}b_{i,k-1}
with
u_{k}\in{\bf Q}_{p}
and
v_{p}(u_{k})=0
or
-1
.
Since
b_{i,j}\in{\mathscr{O}}_{C}
and
b_{i,j}\to 0
when
(i,j)\to\infty
,
it follows that
a_{k}\in p^{-1}{\mathscr{O}}_{C}
and
a_{k}\to 0
when
k\to\infty
.
∎

Remark 4.14
.

The
rank
of an additive element
\ell
is the rank of the
{\bf Z}_{p}
-module

\{(\sigma-1)\ell,\ \sigma\in H_{C\{X\}}\}
.
We have shown that, for all
v\in C
, the additive element
vX\in{\mathbb{W}}_{2}(\overline{C\{X\}})

(which is of rank
0
) admits an additive lifting
\ell_{v}

in
{\mathbb{E}}(\overline{C\{X\}})
of rank
\leq 1
, and it is of rank
0

if and only if
\lambda_{v}=0
, which
is the case if
{\mathbb{E}}
is split.

To prove injectivity in (ii) of Proposition
4.10
,
it suffices to prove that conversely, if
v\neq 0
and
\ell_{v}
is
of rank
0
, then
{\mathbb{E}}
splits.
But
x\mapsto s_{v}(x):=(\tau_{x/v}-1)\ell_{v}
, with
\tau_{x/v}\in T_{\Lambda}
,
x(\tau_{x/v})=x/v
,
gives a functorial splitting
v{\mathscr{O}}_{\Lambda}\to{\mathbb{E}}(\Lambda)

which can be extended, by
{\bf Q}_{p}
-linearity to a splitting
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}(\Lambda)\to{\mathbb{E}}(\Lambda)
.
Indeed, since
\ell_{v}
is of rank
0
,
s_{v}(x):=(\tau_{x/v}-1)\ell_{v}
only depends on

x/v
and is a lifting of
(\tau_{x/v}-1)\cdot vX=x
; functoriality
comes from the fact that, if
\overline{u}:\overline{\Lambda\{X\}}\to\overline{\Lambda^{\prime}\{X\}}

is a lifting of
u:\Lambda\to\Lambda^{\prime}
(if
\overline{u}_{1},\overline{u}_{2}
are two
such liftings, there exists
\sigma\in H_{\Lambda\{X\}}

with
\overline{u}_{2}=\overline{u}_{1}\circ\sigma
; this follows from (iii) of
[
10
, Prop. 5.20]
)
and if
\tau^{\prime}\in T_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
there exists (a unique)
\tau\in T_{\Lambda}

such that
\overline{u}\circ\tau=\tau^{\prime}\circ\overline{u}

(again by
[
10
, Prop. 5.20]
). Applying this to
\tau^{\prime}

with
x(\tau^{\prime})=u(x)/v

produces
\tau
with
x(\tau)=x/v
, and functoriality of
s_{v}
follows
from

u(s_{v}(x))=\overline{u}((\tau-1)\ell_{v})=(\tau^{\prime}-1)\ell_{v}=s_{v}(u(x))

Remark 4.15
.

(i)
Privileging
\psi_{1}
as we did can seem fishy, but
\psi_{\alpha}-\alpha\psi_{1}
is a coboundary
in
\overline{C\{X\}}
(there exists an additive element
f_{\alpha}\in\overline{C\{X\}}
such that

(\sigma-1)f_{\alpha}=\psi_{\alpha}(\sigma)-\alpha\psi_{1}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in H_{C\{X\}}
;
this is a consequence of
[
10
, Th. 10.5]
).

(ii) The space
{\rm Sp}(C\{X\})
is a
K(\pi,1)
(it is the closed unit ball, an affinoid),
and
\overline{C\{X\}}
is (the completion of) a pro-étale perfectoid extension of

C\{X\}
;
hence almost étale descent gives
an isomorphism
H^{1}(H_{C\{X\}},\overline{C\{X\}})\simeq H^{1}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}({\rm Sp}(C\{X\}),{\mathscr{O}})
,
and the proof gives an isomorphism

{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{V}}^{1},{\mathbb{V}}^{1})\simeq H^{0}(T_{C},H^{1}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}({\rm Sp}(C\{X\}),{\mathscr{O}}))\simeq H^{0}(T_{C},C\{X\})=C.

4.3.
Extensions of
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Modules

We say that a topological
C
-vector space is
reasonable

if it is a fréchet or a compact inductive
limit of banachs, and if it is
separable
(i.e., it contains a dense
sub-
C
-vector space of countable dimension).

Let
W\in C_{C}
and let
{\mathbb{W}}:={\mathbb{B}}_{1}\widehat{\otimes}_{C}W
be the
functor
\Lambda\mapsto\Lambda\widehat{\otimes}_{C}W
(as a TVS,
{\mathbb{B}}_{1}\simeq{\mathbb{V}}^{1}
, but we are
going to use
{\mathbb{B}}_{1}
to emphasize that it is also, naturally, a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module).

Remark 4.16
.

If
0\to W_{1}\to W_{2}\to W_{3}\to 0
is a strict exact sequence of reasonable topological
C
-vector spaces then the induced sequence

0\to{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{3}\to 0

is an exact sequence of Topological Vector Spaces. Indeed, it suffices to check that, for sympathetic
\Lambda
, the sequence

0\to\Lambda\widehat{\otimes}_{C}W_{1}\to\Lambda\widehat{\otimes}_{C}W_{2}\to\Lambda\widehat{\otimes}_{C}W_{3}\to 0

is strict exact in
C_{{\bf Q}_{p}}
. But this follows since
W_{*}
’s are reasonable and
\Lambda
is banach (see
[
13
, Sec. 2.1.3]
for a discussion of exactness of the tensor product).

4.3.1.
Extensions of
{\mathbb{B}}_{1}
by
{\mathbb{W}}

Remark 4.17
.

If
W\in C_{C}
and
w\in W
, one builds the pushout extension
E_{w}
of
C
by
W
, using the
diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\textstyle{{\bf B}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{w}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

i.e.,
E_{w}=({\bf B}_{2}\oplus W)/\{(c,cw),\ c\in C\}
.
It is clear from the description that
E_{w}
is a
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module;
the above construction gives an isomorphism
W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Ext}^{1}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(C,W)
.
The associated TVS extension is given by the pushout diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{B}}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{B}}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{B}}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{E}}_{w}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{B}}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
W\in C_{C}
be reasonable.
We have natural isomorphims

{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{B}}_{1},{\mathbb{W}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}{\rm Hom}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(C,W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}W\quad{\rm and}\quad{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{B}}_{1},{\mathbb{W}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}{\rm Ext}^{1}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(C,W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}W.

Proof.

To prove the statement for
{\rm{Hom}}
, since
W\hookrightarrow{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{B}}_{1},{\mathbb{W}})
, it suffices to show that
W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{B}}_{1},{\mathbb{W}})
. Let
\alpha:{\mathbb{B}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{W}}

be a morphism, and let
w=\alpha(1)\in W
. Then, for each
\lambda\in W^{\boldsymbol{*}}
,
the composition
\lambda\circ\alpha:{\mathbb{B}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{B}}_{1}
is the multiplication by
some element of
C
and, evaluating at
1
, one gets that
\lambda\circ\alpha

is the multiplication by
\lambda(w)
. It follows that
\alpha
is the map

a\mapsto aw
which allows to conclude.

Let us turn to
{\rm Ext}^{1}
.
As in the proof of Proposition
4.10
, a TVS-extension

0\to{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{B}}_{1}\to 0
is controled by the
cohomology class of the
1
-cocycle
\sigma\mapsto(\sigma-1)h

on
H_{C\{X\}}
, where
h\in{\mathbb{E}}(\overline{C\{X\}})

is a lifting of
X\in{\mathbb{B}}_{1}(\overline{C\{X\}})
.
Since all the steps in the proof of Proposition
4.10
(including Lemma
4.13
)
involve bounded denominators,
the proof of Proposition
4.10
goes through, using orthonormal bases,
to treat the case of
{\mathbb{W}}
being a (separable) banach.

The case of nuclear fréchets follows from
the case of banachs by taking projective limits (one can write a fréchet
W

as a projective limits of banachs
W_{n}
), noticing that

{\rm Rlim}^{1}{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{B}}_{1},{\mathbb{B}}_{1}\widehat{\otimes}W_{n})={\rm Rlim}^{1}W_{n}

vanishes by the topological
Mittag-Leffler criterium.

If
W=\varinjlim_{n}W_{n}
, use the fact that
H_{C\{X\}}
is profinite which implies that any
continuous
1
-cocycle
\sigma\mapsto c_{\sigma}

on
H_{C\{X\}}
with values in
W
takes values in
W_{n_{0}}
for some big enough
n_{0}

(Lemma
4.19
); this
allows to reduce this case to the banach case.
∎

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
H
be a profinite group, and
W=\varinjlim_{n}W_{n}
a compact inductive limit of
C
-banachs,
with
H
acting continuously on
W_{n}
by isometries.
If
\sigma\mapsto c_{\sigma}
is a continuous cocycle on
H
with values in
W
, there exists

n_{0}\in{\bf N}
such that
c_{\sigma}\in W_{n_{0}}
for all
\sigma\in H
.

Proof.

The topological closure
B_{n}
inside
W
of the unit ball of
W_{n}
is
c
-compact, hence

U_{n}:=\sum_{i=0}^{n}p^{i-n}B_{n}
is a closed subgroup of
W
, and
W=\cup_{n}U_{n}
.
If
H_{n}=\{\sigma\in H,c_{\sigma}\in U_{n}\}
, then
H_{n}
is subgroup of
H

(because of the cocycle condition and the fact that
H
acts via isometries), which is closed
since
U_{n}
is closed,
and
H=\cup_{n}H_{n}
. It follows, by Baire,
that there exists
n_{1}
such that
H_{n_{1}}
has a non empty
interior, hence is open since it is a subgroup,
and is of finite index since
H
is profinite.
Take
n_{0}\geq n_{1}
such that
W_{n_{0}}
contains
c_{\sigma}

for
\sigma
in a system of representatives
of
H/H_{n_{1}}
; the cocycle relation implies that
c_{\sigma}\in W_{n_{0}}

for all
\sigma\in H
.
∎

4.3.2.
Extensions of
{\mathbb{B}}_{d}
by a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module

Note that
{\bf B}_{m}
is naturally a banach algebra (with ring of integers the image of
{\bf A}_{{\rm inf}}
).
We say that a topological
{\bf B}_{m}
-module is reasonable
if it is a
{\bf B}_{m}
-fréchet
(i.e., the topology is defined by a countable family of norms of
{\bf B}_{m}
-modules)
or a compact (or rather
c
-compact) inductive
limit of
{\bf B}_{m}
-banachs.

Let
W
be a topological
{\bf B}_{m}
-module,
and let
{\mathbb{W}}:={\mathbb{B}}_{m}\widehat{\otimes}_{{\bf B}_{m}}W
be the
functor
\Lambda\mapsto{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)\widehat{\otimes}_{{\bf B}_{m}}W
.

Remark 4.20
.

If
0\to W_{1}\to W_{2}\to W_{3}\to 0
is a strict exact sequence of reasonable topological
{\bf B}_{m}
-modules then the induced sequence

0\to{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{3}\to 0

is an exact sequence of
{\mathbb{B}}_{m}
-Modules. Indeed, it suffices to check that, for sympathetic
\Lambda
, the sequence

0\to{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)\widehat{\otimes}_{{\bf B}_{m}}W_{1}\to{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)\widehat{\otimes}_{{\bf B}_{m}}W\to{\mathbb{B}}_{m}(\Lambda)\widehat{\otimes}_{{\bf B}_{m}}W_{2}\to 0

is strict exact in
C_{{\bf Q}_{p}}
. But this follows by reduction to
m=1
, where it is clear because
W_{*}
’s are reasonable and
\Lambda
is banach.

Remark 4.21
.

If
W
is a topological
{\bf B}_{m}
-module and
w\in W
, one builds the pushout extension
E_{w}
of
{\bf B}_{d}
by
W
, using the
diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\bf B}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\textstyle{{\bf B}_{m+d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\bf B}_{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{w}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\bf B}_{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

i.e.,
E_{w}=({\bf B}_{m+d}\oplus W)/\{(t^{d}c,cw),\ c\in{\bf B}_{m}\}
.
It is clear from the description that
E_{w}
is a
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module; the extension splits if

w=t^{d}w^{\prime}
and
the above construction gives an isomorphism
W/t^{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Ext}^{1}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}({\bf B}_{d},W)
.
The associated TVS extension is given by the diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{B}}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{B}}_{m+d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{B}}_{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{E}}_{w}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{B}}_{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
W
be a reasonable
{\bf B}_{m}
-module.
We have natural isomorphisms

{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{B}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}{\rm Hom}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}({\bf B}_{d},W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}W[t^{d}]\quad{\rm and}\quad{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{B}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}{\rm Ext}^{1}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}({\bf B}_{d},W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}W/t^{d},

where
W[t^{i}]
denotes the
t^{i}
-torsion submodule of
W
.

Proof.

We argue by dévissage, first on
m
(we have the exact sequence

0\to W[t]\to W\to W/W[t]\to 0
; if
W
is a reasonable topological
{\bf B}_{m}
-module,

W[t]
and
W/W[t]
are reasonable topological
{\bf B}_{m-1}
-modules), then on
d
.
The case
d=m=1
is covered by Proposition
4.18
and Remark
4.21
.

\bullet
Let
0\to W_{1}\to W\to W_{2}\to 0
be an exact sequence of reasonable
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-modules
with
W_{1},W_{2}
killed by
t^{m-1}
,
and let

be the corresponding
sequence of TVS’s. Set

.
We have a commutative diagram with exact rows:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{1}[t]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{W[t]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{2}[t]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{W_{1}/t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{W/t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{2}/t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{0}({\mathbb{W}}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{0}({\mathbb{W}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{0}({\mathbb{W}}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{h^{1}({\mathbb{W}}_{2})}

where the vertical isomorphisms come from the induction hypothesis.
It follows that the remaining two vertical arrows are also isomorphisms, which allows to run the induction on
m
for
d=1
.

\bullet
Now, use the
long exact sequence of
{\rm Ext}
groups for the exact sequence

.
and set

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the vertical isomorphisms come from the induction hypothesis.
It follows that the remaining two vertical arrows are also isomorphisms, which allows to run the induction on
