.

This concludes the proof.
∎

4.4.
The filtration on a qBC

Let
{\mathbb{W}}=({\mathbb{W}}_{1},{\mathbb{W}}_{2})
be a reasonable
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pair. That is, both
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
are reasonable
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Modules and of the same type
(i.e., both fréchets or both of compact type).

Lemma 4.23
.

We have a natural isomorphism

{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{B}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}(W_{2}/W_{1})[t^{d}]
.

Proof.

We have the following diagram with exact rows, in which the middle row is
computed in the left heart of the category of topological
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-modules.
The first row is the
the beginning of the long exact sequence
of
{\rm Ext}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}({\bf B}_{d},-)
(without topology)
associated to the exact sequence
0\to W_{1}\to W_{2}\to W_{2}/W_{1}\to 0
.
And
{\rm Hom}
and
{\rm Ext}^{1}
in the last row stand for

{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}
and
{\rm Ext}_{\rm TVS}^{1}
.

\textstyle{W_{1}[t^{d}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{W_{2}[t^{d}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{(W_{2}/W_{1})[t^{d}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{1}/t^{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{W_{2}/t^{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}({{\bf B}}_{d},{W}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}({{\bf B}}_{d},{W}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}({{\bf B}}_{d},{W})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}^{1}({{\bf B}}_{d},{W}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}^{1}({{\bf B}}_{d},{W}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}({\mathbb{B}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}({\mathbb{B}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}({\mathbb{B}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathbb{B}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathbb{B}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}}_{2})}

By Proposition
4.22
(and Remark
4.21
),
all vertical arrows are isomorphisms except the middle ones.
We conclude by the 5 Lemma that the same is true for the middle arrows.
∎

Lemma 4.24
.

(i)

If
{\mathbb{W}}
is a reasonable
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module and
W={\mathbb{W}}(C)
, then

{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{U}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}W,\quad{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{U}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}W,\quad d\in{\bf N}.

(ii)
If
{\mathbb{W}}=({\mathbb{W}}_{1},{\mathbb{W}}_{2})
is a reasonable
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pair,
then

{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{U}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}W_{2}/W_{1}.

Proof.

For (i),
choose
m
such that
t^{m}
kills
{\mathbb{W}}
. The injection
{\mathbb{U}}_{d}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{B}}_{m+d}

gives rise to a commutative diagram with exact rows, in which
h^{0}(-):={\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}(-,{\mathbb{W}})

and
h^{1}(-):={\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm TVS}(-,{\mathbb{W}})

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{0}({\mathbb{B}}_{d})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{0}({\mathbb{U}}_{d})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{0}({\bf Q}_{p}t^{d})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{1}({\mathbb{B}}_{d})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{1}({\mathbb{U}}_{d})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{1}({\bf Q}_{p}t^{d})}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{0}({\mathbb{B}}_{d})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{0}({\mathbb{B}}_{d+m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{0}(t^{d}{\mathbb{B}}_{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{1}({\mathbb{B}}_{d})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{1}({\mathbb{B}}_{d+m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{h^{1}(t^{d}{\mathbb{B}}_{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W[t^{d}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{t^{d}}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{W/t^{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}

The isomorphisms between the second and third rows come from Proposition
4.22

and the assumption that
t^{m}
kills
{\mathbb{W}}
.

Now,
h^{1}({\bf Q}_{p}t^{d})=0

and
h^{0}(t^{d}{\mathbb{B}}_{m})\to h^{0}({\bf Q}_{p}t^{d})
is an isomorphism.
Hence
h^{0}({\bf Q}_{p}t^{d})\to h^{1}({\mathbb{B}}_{d})
is surjective, and
h^{1}({\mathbb{U}}_{d})=0
.
An application of the
5
lemma shows that
h^{0}({\mathbb{B}}_{d+m})\to h^{0}({\mathbb{U}}_{d})
is
an isomorphism which finishes the proof of (i).

Since (ii) is a direct consequence of (i) and the exact sequence of Ext’s, this concludes
the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.25
.

If
{\mathbb{Y}}
is a BC of curvature
>0
and if
{\mathbb{W}}
is a
reasonable
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pair,
then
{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{Y}},{\mathbb{W}})=0
.

Proof.

{\mathbb{Y}}
is a direct sum of BC’s of the form
{\mathbb{B}}_{d}/{\bf Q}_{p^{h}}
or
{\mathbb{U}}_{d}/{\bf Q}_{p}t_{x}^{d}

with
x\neq\infty
(i.e.
\theta(t_{x})\neq 0
), hence we may assume

{\mathbb{Y}}
to be of one of these forms.

\bullet
If
{\mathbb{Y}}={\mathbb{B}}_{d}/{\bf Q}_{p^{h}}
, we
have an exact sequence:

0\to{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{Y}},{\mathbb{W}})\to{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{B}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}})\to{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\bf Q}_{p^{h}},{\mathbb{W}})

and it is enough to show the injectivity of the last map.
We have
{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\bf Q}_{p^{h}},{\mathbb{W}})={\mathbb{W}}(C)^{\oplus h}

and Lemma
4.23
shows that

{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{B}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}})={\mathbb{W}}(C)[t^{d}]
which injects into
{\mathbb{W}}(C)={\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\bf Q}_{p},{\mathbb{W}})

which is a quotient of
{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\bf Q}_{p^{h}},{\mathbb{W}})
.

\bullet
If
{\mathbb{Y}}={\mathbb{U}}_{d}/{\bf Q}_{p}t_{x}^{d}
, we
have an exact sequence:

0\to{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{Y}},{\mathbb{W}})\to{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{U}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}})\to{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\bf Q}_{p}t_{x}^{d},{\mathbb{W}})

The induced map
W\to W
coming from
{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{U}}_{d},{\mathbb{W}})\simeq{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\bf Q}_{p},{\mathbb{W}})=W
is multiplication by
t_{x}^{d}
, hence
is an isomorphism since
\theta(t_{x})\neq 0
. It follows
that
{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{Y}},{\mathbb{W}})=0
, as wanted.
∎

Proposition 4.26
.

Let
{\mathbb{W}}
be a reasonable qBC, i.e.,
{\mathbb{W}}_{=0}
is a reasonable
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-pair.

(i)

{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}
is the largest sub-BC of
{\mathbb{W}}
of curvature
>0
.

(ii)

{\mathbb{W}}_{<0}
is the largest quotient-BC of
{\mathbb{W}}
of curvature
<0
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathbb{Y}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{W}}
be a BC of curvature
>0
.
The map
{\mathbb{Y}}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{<0}
is
0
by Remark
3.6
, hence
{\mathbb{Y}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{W}}_{\geq 0}
. But the induced map
{\mathbb{Y}}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{=0}
is
0

by Proposition
4.25
, hence
{\mathbb{Y}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}
,
which proves (i).

Now, let
{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{Y}}
be a surjective map of TVS’s, with
{\mathbb{Y}}

a BC of curvature
<0
. As above, the restriction
{\mathbb{W}}_{>0}\to{\mathbb{Y}}

is
0
, hence
{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{Y}}
factors through
{\mathbb{W}}_{\leq 0}
.

If
{\mathbb{W}}_{=0}=({\mathbb{W}}_{1},{\mathbb{W}}_{2})
, we
have
{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{W}}_{=0},{\mathbb{Y}})\hookrightarrow{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{W}}_{2},{\mathbb{Y}})
,
and a morphism
{\mathbb{W}}_{=0}\to{\mathbb{Y}}
as above is
0

on any sub-
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module of finite length of
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
; since the union of
these modules is dense in
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
, the induced morphism

{\mathbb{W}}_{2}\to{\mathbb{Y}}
is identically
0
,
and so is
{\mathbb{W}}_{=0}\to{\mathbb{Y}}
;
hence
{\mathbb{W}}\to{\mathbb{Y}}
factors through
{\mathbb{W}}_{<0}
,
which proves (ii).
∎

4.5.
Morphisms of qBC’s

We show here that morphisms between reasonable qBCs of the same type behave as expected.

4.5.1.
Extensions of reasonable
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Modules

Proposition 4.27
.

If
{W}_{1}
and
{W_{2}}
are reasonable topological
{\bf B}_{m}
-modules of the same type,
and
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{W}_{2}}
are the associated topological
{\mathbb{B}}_{m}
-Modules,
the natural maps

(4.28)

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(W_{2},W_{1})\to{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}({\mathbb{W}}_{2}

\displaystyle,{\mathbb{W}}_{1})\to{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{W}}_{2},{\mathbb{W}}_{1})

\displaystyle{\rm Ext}^{1}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(W_{2},W_{1})\to{\rm Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}({\mathbb{W}}_{2}

\displaystyle,{\mathbb{W}}_{1})\to{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{W}}_{2},{\mathbb{W}}_{1})

are isomorphisms.

Proof.

Let
\alpha:{\mathbb{W}}_{2}\to{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
be a morphism of TVS’s. To prove
the statement
about Hom’s we just have to check that
\alpha

is
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-linear. Linearity can be tested on finite rank
submodules, and a finite rank sub-
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module
Y
of
W_{2}
is of the form

{\bf B}_{d_{1}}e_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\bf B}_{d_{s}}e_{s}
, hence the sub-
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module
{\mathbb{Y}}
generated
by
Y
is
{\mathbb{B}}_{d_{1}}e_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathbb{B}}_{d_{s}}e_{s}
. It follows
from Proposition
4.22
that the restriction of
\alpha
to
{\mathbb{Y}}

is
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-linear and the restriction of
\alpha_{C}
to
Y
is

{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-linear. This proves that
\alpha_{C}
is
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-linear,
and that for any sympathetic
\Lambda
the restriction of
\alpha_{\Lambda}

to
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}(\Lambda)\otimes_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}W_{2}
is
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}(\Lambda)
-linear.
Since
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}(\Lambda)\otimes_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}W_{2}
is dense in
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}(\Lambda)
,
and since
\alpha_{\Lambda}
is continuous, it follows that
\alpha_{\Lambda}

is
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}(\Lambda)
-linear, and that
\alpha
is
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-linear, as wanted.

The statement about
{\rm Ext}^{1}
’s is much more delicate.
Let
{\mathbb{W}}={\mathbb{W}}_{0}
be a TVS-extension of
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
by
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
, and set
W:={\mathbb{W}}(C)
.
It follows from Proposition
4.22
that
W
is naturally a
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module:
indeed it is enough to check that, for elements
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}
of
W_{2}
, if

Y
is the
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-submodule they generate, then the inverse image
\widetilde{Y}
of
Y

in
W
is a
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module; but
Y={\bf B}_{d_{1}}e_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\bf B}_{d_{s}}e_{s}

and if we let
{\mathbb{Y}}
be the sub-
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module

{\mathbb{B}}_{d_{1}}e_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathbb{B}}_{d_{s}}e_{s}
of
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
,
Proposition
4.22
implies that its inverse image
\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}
in

{\mathbb{W}}
is a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Module; hence
\widetilde{Y}=\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}(C)
is a
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module.
This gives a map

{\rm Res}_{C}:{\rm Ext}^{1}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{W}}_{2},{\mathbb{W}}_{1})\to{\rm Ext}^{1}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(W_{2},W_{1})

inverse to the natural map, which proves that
{\rm Res}_{C}
is surjective.

It remains to show that
{\rm Res}_{C}
is injective. That is to say: if

{\mathbb{W}}
is an TVS-extension of
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
by
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
, and if

W\to W_{2}
admits a continuous
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-linear section, then
{\mathbb{W}}
splits.

First assume that
W_{1},W_{2}
are killed by
t
(i.e. are topological
C
-vector spaces).
If
i=0,1,2
, let
\ell({\mathbb{W}}_{i})
be the set of additive elements of rank
0
of

{\mathbb{W}}_{i}(\overline{C\{X\}})
, i.e. the set of
\ell\in{\mathbb{W}}_{i}(\overline{C\{X\}})
,
such that
\ell(\tau)=0
if
\tau\in H_{C\{X\}}
and
\ell(\sigma\tau)=\ell(\sigma)+\ell(\tau)

for all
\sigma,\tau\in T_{C}
.
Since
\ell({\mathbb{B}}_{1})=\{cX,\ c\in C\}
, the map
v\mapsto\ell_{v}=Xv
gives an isomorphism

W_{i}\overset{\sim}{\to}\ell({\mathbb{W}}_{i})
, if
i=1,2
.

In the other direction,
note that, if
\ell\in\ell({\mathbb{W}}_{i})
, then

\tau\mapsto\ell(\tau)
is a continuous group morphism from

T_{C}
to
W_{i}
, which factors through
T_{C}/H_{C\{X\}}={\mathscr{O}}_{C}
, hence can be
seen as a continuous morphism of
{\bf Z}_{p}
modules
\ell:{\mathscr{O}}_{C}\to W_{i}
.
If
i=1,2
, then
\ell\mapsto\ell(1)
from
\ell({\mathbb{W}}_{i})
to
W_{i}

is the inverse of the above map.

Now,

\ell({\mathbb{W}})\to\ell({\mathbb{W}}_{2})
is surjective (if
v\in W_{2}
and
\ell_{v}=Xv
,
Remark
4.14
– adapted to the setting of Proposition
4.18
–
produces
\ell\in{\mathbb{W}}(\overline{C\{X\}})
lifting
\ell_{v}
,
additive of rank
\leq 1
, and of rank
1
if and only if the extension
of
Cv
by
W_{1}
is not split (as a
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module)).
Hence
\ell\mapsto\ell(1)
gives rise to an isomorphism

\ell({\mathbb{W}})\overset{\sim}{\to}W
.
But
{\mathbb{W}}(\overline{C\{X\}})
is reasonable (cf. Appendix
A
),
and
\ell({\mathbb{W}})
is closed in
{\mathbb{W}}(\overline{C\{X\}})

as can be seen from the definition. It follows that
\ell({\mathbb{W}})

is also reasonable, and the open mapping theorem implies that
the above isomorphism is a topological isomorphism.
Composing the section
W_{2}\to W
with the inverse of the above isomorphism
produces a continuous
{\bf Q}_{p}
-linear map

v\mapsto\ell_{v}
from
W_{2}
to
\ell({\mathbb{W}})
,
with the property that
\ell_{v}(1)=v\in W_{2}\subset W
.
This also defines a splitting
\ell({\mathbb{W}})=\ell({\mathbb{W}}_{1})\oplus\ell({\mathbb{W}}_{2})
,
which allow to
define a
C
-module structure on
\ell({\mathbb{W}})
by setting
c\cdot\ell_{v}=\ell_{cv}

(
\ell({\mathbb{W}}_{1})
is already a
C
-module).
Then, by definition,
(c\cdot\ell_{v})(x)=x\,cv=c\,xv=c\,\ell_{v}(x)\in W_{2}
, if
x\in{\mathscr{O}}_{C}
.

Let now
\Lambda
be a sympathetic algebra.
The action of
H_{\Lambda\{X\}}
on
\ell_{v}
factors through
H_{C\{X\}}
since

\ell_{v}\in{\mathbb{W}}(\overline{C\{X\}})
; hence it is trivial.
It follows that

\tau\mapsto s_{0}(\tau(\ell_{v}))
factors through
{\mathscr{O}}_{\Lambda}
and defines
a continuous function
x\mapsto\ell_{v}(x)
on
{\mathscr{O}}_{\Lambda}
, with values
in
{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)
.
We will need the following lemma.

Lemma 4.29
.

\ell_{v}:{\mathscr{O}}_{\Lambda}\to{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)
is a continuous group morphism;
moreover it is
C
-linear in the sense that

\ell_{v}(cx)=(c\cdot\ell_{v})(x)
for all
c\in{\mathscr{O}}_{C}

and
x\in{\mathscr{O}}_{\Lambda}
.

Proof.

Let
\iota_{\Lambda}:\overline{C\{X\}}\to\overline{\Lambda\{X\}}
be the
natural injection. Then

\tau\mapsto(\tau-1)\iota_{\Lambda}(\ell_{v})
is a continuous
1
-cocycle
on
T_{\Lambda}
with values in

{\mathbb{W}}(\overline{\Lambda\{X\}})
. Now, if
\alpha:\Lambda\to C
is continuous,
we can extend
\alpha
(non uniquely) to
\tilde{\alpha}:\overline{\Lambda\{X\}}\to\overline{C\{X\}}
with
\tilde{\alpha}(X)=X
.
In particular
\tilde{\alpha}\circ\tau\circ\iota_{\Lambda}\in T_{C}
and its image in
{\mathscr{O}}_{C}

is
\alpha(x(\tau))
(remember that
x(\tau)\in\Lambda
).

Then
\tilde{\alpha}((\tau-1)\circ\iota_{\Lambda}(\ell_{v}))=\ell_{v}(\alpha(x(\tau))
.
It follows that
(\tau-1)\iota_{\Lambda}(\ell_{v})-\ell_{v}(x(\tau))
is killed by

\tilde{\alpha}
for all
\tilde{\alpha}
, which implies that it is
0
.
Hence the above cocycle is
\tau\mapsto\ell(x(\tau))
and is a morphism of
groups since
T_{\Lambda}
acts trivially on
{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)
.
This proves the first statement.

To prove the second statement,
let
c\in{\mathscr{O}}_{C}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
, and choose
an isomorphism
\tilde{c}:\overline{\Lambda\{X\}}\to\overline{\Lambda\{X\}}

with
\tilde{c}(X)=cX
(this implies that
\tilde{c}
induces an automorphism
of
\overline{C\{X\}}
). Note that, if
\tilde{c}_{1},\tilde{c}_{2}
are two such isomorphisms,
then
\tilde{c}_{1}^{-1}\circ\tilde{c}_{2}\in H_{\Lambda\{X\}}
. It follows that
\tilde{c}(\ell_{v})

only depends on
c
and not on the choice of
\tilde{c}
.
Fix also
\alpha_{\Lambda}:\overline{\Lambda\{X\}}\to\Lambda
with
\alpha_{\Lambda}(X)=0

Let us check that
\tilde{c}(\ell_{v})=\ell_{cv}
.
If
\tau\in T_{\Lambda}
, then
\tilde{c}^{-1}\tau\tilde{c}\in T_{\Lambda}
and

\displaystyle x(\tilde{c}^{-1}\tau\tilde{c})

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\Lambda}(\tilde{c}^{-1}\tau\tilde{c}(X))=\alpha_{\Lambda}(\tilde{c}^{-1}\tau(cX))

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\Lambda}(\tilde{c}^{-1}(c(X+x(\tau))))=\alpha_{\Lambda}(X+c\,x(\tau))=c\,x(\tau)

Hence

\tau(\tilde{c}(\ell_{v}))=\tilde{c}(\tilde{c}^{-1}\tau\tilde{c}(\ell_{v}))=\tilde{c}(\ell_{v}+\ell_{v}(c\,x(\tau)))=\tilde{c}(\ell_{v})+\ell_{v}(c\,x(\tau))

Now, if
\Lambda=C
, then
\ell_{v}(c\,x(\tau))=c\,x(\tau)\,v=\ell_{cv}(x(\tau))

It follows that
\tilde{c}(\ell_{v})-\ell_{cv}
is invariant by
T_{C}
, hence belongs to

W
. Moreover,
\alpha_{C}\circ\tilde{c}:\overline{C\{X\}}\to C
sends
X
to
0
,
hence there existe
\sigma\in H_{C\{X\}}
such that
\alpha_{C}\circ\tilde{c}=\alpha_{C}\circ\sigma
;
it follows that
\alpha_{C}(\tilde{c}(\ell_{v}))=\alpha_{C}(\ell_{v})=0
since
\ell_{v}
is additive.
Since
\alpha_{C}(\ell_{cv})=0
because
\ell_{cv}
is also additive, we get that

\alpha_{C}(\tilde{c}(\ell_{v})-\ell_{cv})=0
, hence
\tilde{c}(\ell_{v})-\ell_{cv}=0
.
Hence

\ell_{v}(c\,x(\tau))=(\tau-1)\cdot(\tilde{c}(\ell_{v}))=(\tau-1)\cdot\ell_{cv}=\ell_{cv}(x(\tau))

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Let us come back to the proof of the proposition:
we are going to use the lemma to split
{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)\to{\mathbb{W}}_{2}(\Lambda)
.
Let
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
be an “orthornormal” basis of
{\mathscr{O}}_{\Lambda}
over
{\mathscr{O}}_{C}

(i.e.
\inf_{i}{v_{p}(x_{i})}\leq v_{\Lambda}(\sum_{i}x_{i}e_{i})\leq 1+\inf_{i}{v_{p}(x_{i})}
).
Then any element of
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}(\Lambda)
can be written uniquely as

\sum_{i}e_{i}v_{i}
, with
v_{i}\in W_{2}
tending to
0
when
i\to\infty
.
Now
\ell_{v_{i}}(e_{i})\to 0
in
{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)
(one can find
k_{i}\in{\bf N}
, with
k_{i}\to\infty
,
such that
p^{-k_{i}}v_{i}
is bounded in
W_{2}
, and
s_{i}:\overline{C\{X\}}\to\Lambda

with
s_{i}(X)=e_{i}
, then
p^{-k_{i}}\ell_{v_{i}}(e_{i})=s_{i}(\ell_{p^{-k_{i}}v_{i}})
is bounded
in
{\mathbb{W}}(\Lambda)
, and
\ell_{v_{i}}(e_{i})\to 0
).
Hence we can set
s(\sum_{i}e_{i}v_{i})=\sum_{i}\ell_{v_{i}}(e_{i})
.
Then we have
s(\sum_{j}f_{j}w_{j})=\sum_{j}\ell_{w_{j}}(f_{j})
for any
family
(f_{j})_{j\in J}
bounded in
\Lambda
and any family

(w_{j})_{j\in J}
converging to
0
in
W_{2}
. Indeed,
write
f_{j}=\sum_{i}a_{i,j}e_{i}
and
v_{i}=\sum_{i}a_{i,j}w_{j}
(then
a_{i,j}
is bounded
and, for all
j
,
a_{i,j}\to 0
when
i\to\infty
, which implies that

v_{i}\to 0
when
i\to\infty
).
Then
\sum_{j}f_{j}w_{j}=\sum_{i,j}a_{i,j}e_{i}w_{j}=\sum_{i}e_{i}v_{i}
.
But

\sum_{j}\ell_{w_{j}}(f_{j})=\sum_{j}\ell_{w_{j}}(\sum_{i}a_{i,j}e_{i})=\sum_{i,j}\ell_{a_{i,j}w_{j}}(e_{i})=\sum_{i}\ell_{\sum_{j}a_{i,j}w_{j}}(e_{i})=\sum_{i}\ell_{v_{i}}(e_{i})

This shows,
in particular, that
s
does not depend on the choice of
(e_{i})_{i}
.
It also implies that
s
is functorial in
\Lambda
. This finishes the proof
of injectivity of
{\rm Res}_{C}

in the case
W_{1},W_{2}
killed by
t
.

Injectivity of
{\rm Res}_{C}
in
the general case follows by dévissage, using the long exact sequences of

{\rm Ext}
’s and arguing by induction first on
m_{1}
, then on
m_{2}
,
if
W_{i}
is killed by
t^{m_{i}}
.
∎

4.5.2.
Morphisms of reasonable
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pairs

Theorem 4.30
.

Let
{\mathbb{W}}=({\mathbb{W}}_{1},{\mathbb{W}}_{2})
and
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}=({\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}_{1},{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}_{2})
be
reasonable
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-Pairs of the same type, and let
W,W^{\prime}
be the associated
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-pairs. Then the natural maps

{\rm Hom}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(W,W^{\prime})\to{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime})\to{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime})

are isomorphisms.

Proof.

The short exact sequence (in
{\rm LH}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})
)

0\to W_{1}\to W_{2}\to W\to 0

yields the isomorphism

{\rm Hom}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(W,W^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Ker}\big({\rm Hom}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(W_{2},W^{\prime})\to{\rm Hom}_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(W_{1},W^{\prime})\big).

Similarly for
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}
and
{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}
.
Hence, we are reduced to showing the claim of the theorem for
W_{1},W_{2}
;
in other words, we may suppose
W
to be a reasonable
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module.

Now we have a map of long exact sequences (in the first line we work in the category of
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-pairs
and in the second in the category of Topological Vector Spaces):

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}({W},{W^{\prime}}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}({W},{W^{\prime}}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}({W},{W^{\prime}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}^{1}({W},{W^{\prime}}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}^{1}({W},{W^{\prime}}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{W}^{\prime}}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{W}^{\prime}}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{W}^{\prime}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{W}^{\prime}}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{W}^{\prime}}_{2})}

with all vertical arrows isomorphisms by Proposition
4.27
except the middle one.
It follows, thanks to the 5 Lemma, that the middle
arrow is also an isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 4.31
.

Let
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime\prime}
be a morphism of TVS’s where
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime},{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime\prime}
are qBCs of the same type.
Then
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}_{>0}
maps to
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime\prime}_{>0}
,
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}_{\geq 0}
maps to
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime\prime}_{\geq 0}
,
and the induced map
{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime}_{=0}\to{\mathbb{W}}^{\prime\prime}_{=0}
is
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-linear.

Proof.

The first part of the claim is proved in the same way as Proposition
4.26
.
The last part follows from Theorem
4.30
.
∎

5.
Filtered
(\varphi,N)
-modules

In this chapter we study filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-modules
over
K
or
C
and their relations to the categories of almost
C
-representations
and BC’s. In particular,
we introduce the notion of acyclic
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-modules
as a generalization of weakly-admissible
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-modules.
While the
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-modules
(H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X_{C}),H^{i}_{\rm dR}(X))

coming from algebraic geometry
tend to be weakly admissible those coming from overconvergent geometry tend to
be only acyclic (as shown later in this paper).

The results of this chapter will be crucial for the proofs of our results towards
the
C_{\rm st}
-conjecture. In particular, Theorem
5.8
and its corollaries
(resp. Proposition
5.20
)
will be used to study the pro-étale-to-de Rham part of the
C_{\rm st}
-conjecture
for varieties over
K
(resp. over
C
).

5.1.
Filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-modules over
K

5.1.1.
Filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-modules

\bullet

(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-modules
.
A
(\varphi,N)
-module over
F
or
F^{\rm nr}

is a finite dimensional
F
-module or
F^{\rm nr}
-module
M
endowed with a bijective Frobenius

\varphi:M\to M
, semilinear with respect to the absolute Frobenius on
F
or
F^{\rm nr}
, and a
linear map
N:M\to M
satisfying
N\varphi=p\,\varphi N
.

More generally, a
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module over
F^{\rm nr}

is a
(\varphi,N)
-module over
F^{\rm nr}
endowed
with a smooth
13
13

13

This means that the stabilizers of elements of
M
are
open in
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
.
semilinear action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
which commutes with
\varphi
and
N
.

If
M
is a
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module over
F
or
F^{\rm nr}
, we define its dual
M^{\boldsymbol{*}}

as
{\rm Hom}_{F}(M,F)
endowed with actions of

\varphi
,
N
and
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
given by

\langle\varphi(\mu),v\rangle=\varphi(\langle\mu,\varphi^{-1}(v)\rangle),\quad\langle N(\mu),v\rangle=-\langle\mu,N(v)\rangle,\quad\langle\sigma(\mu),v\rangle=\sigma(\langle\mu,\sigma^{-1}(v)\rangle),\ {\text{if $\sigma\in{\mathscr{G}}_{K}$.}}

\bullet

Filtered modules
.
A filtered module
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
over
K
is a
K
-module
M

together with a descending filtration
{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}
on
M
by sub-
K
-modules

{\rm Fil}^{i}M
, with
{\rm Fil}^{i}M=M
if
i\ll 0

and
{\rm Fil}^{i}M=0
if
i\gg 0
.

If
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
is a filtered module over
K
,
we define the dual filtered module
(M^{\boldsymbol{*}},{\rm Fil}_{\perp}^{\bullet})

by endowing
the
K
-dual
M^{\boldsymbol{*}}={\rm Hom}_{K}(M,K)
of
M
, with the filtration

{\rm Fil}_{\perp}^{i}M^{\boldsymbol{*}}=({\rm Fil}^{1-i}M)^{\perp}.

If the filtration is obvious from the context, we don’t indicate it in the notation; for example,
the de Rham cohomology of a variety
X
over
K
is a filtered module over
K
if we
endow it with the Hodge filtration, and will just be denoted by
H^{\bullet}_{\rm dR}(X)
,
the Hodge filtration being taken for granted.

\bullet

Filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-modules
.
A filtered

(\varphi,N)
-module
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
over
K

is a
(\varphi,N)
-module
M
over
F
with
a structure of filtered module over
K
on
M_{K}=M\otimes_{F}K
.

A filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
over
K

is a
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module
M
over
F^{\rm nr}
with
a structure of filtered module over
K
on
M_{K}=(M\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}\overline{K})^{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}
.

If
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
is a filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module over
K
,
we define its dual
(M^{\boldsymbol{*}},{\rm Fil}_{\perp}^{\bullet})
as the
dual
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module
M^{\boldsymbol{*}}
with the module

M_{K}^{\boldsymbol{*}}=(M_{K})^{\boldsymbol{*}}
endowed with the filtration
{\rm Fil}_{\perp}^{\bullet}
.

As before, if the filtration is obvious from the context, we will use sometimes
just
M
to denote a filtered

(\varphi,N)
-module
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
over
K
or sometimes

(M,M_{K})
as in the case of de Rham cohomology:
if
X
is a smooth quasi-compact dagger variety over
K
then
(H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X_{C}),H^{i}_{\rm dR}(X))
is a
filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module over
K
thanks to the Hyodo-Kato isomorphism.

5.1.2.
Acyclicity and admissibility

If
M
is a filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module over
K
,
the rank
{\rm rk}(M)

of
M
is the dimension of
M
over
F^{\rm nr}
.
If
M
has rank
1
, one
defines the
degree
\deg(M)
of
M
by the formula

\deg(M):=t_{N}(M)-t_{H}(M),

where
t_{N}(M)
et
t_{H}(M)
are defined by choosing a basis
e
of
M
over
F^{\rm nr}
:

\bullet
there exists

\lambda\in(F^{\rm nr})^{\boldsymbol{*}}
such that
\varphi(e)=\lambda e
, and we set
t_{N}(M)=v_{p}(\lambda)
;

\bullet

there exists
i\in{\bf Z}
, unique, such that
e\in M_{K}^{i}-M_{K}^{i+1}
, and we set

t_{H}(M)=i
.

If
M
has rank
r\geq 2
, then
\det M=\wedge^{r}M
is of rank
1
, and one defines

the degree of
M
by:

\displaystyle\deg(M):=

\displaystyle\ \deg(\det M)=t_{N}(M)-t_{H}(M),

\displaystyle t_{N}(M):=t_{N}(\det(M)),\quad

\displaystyle t_{H}(M):=t_{H}(\det M)=\sum_{i\in{\bf Z}}i\dim_{K}M_{K}^{i}/M_{K}^{i+1}.

Endowed with the rank and degree functions,
the category of filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-modules over
K
is a
Harder-Narasimhan
\otimes
-category.

Definition 5.1
.

A filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module over
K
is said to be
weakly admissible

if it is semi-stable of slope
0
(a reformulation
[
21
]

of the original notion
[
23
]
).
It is said to be
acyclic
if its Harder-Narasimhan slopes are
\geq 0
.

Remark 5.2
.

A weakly admissible filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module is acyclic; conversely
an acyclic filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module is weakly admissible if and only if
it is of degree
0
.

Lemma 5.3
.

The following conditions are equivalent for a filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module

(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
over
K
:

(a)

(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
is acyclic.

(b)
There exists a filtration
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet}
on
M_{K}
such that

{\rm Fil}_{1}^{i}M_{K}\subset{\rm Fil}^{i}M_{K}
for all
i
, and
(M,{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})

is weakly admissible.

Proof.

To prove (a)
\Rightarrow
(b),
it is enough to show that one can find a filtration
such that
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{i}M_{K}\subset{\rm Fil}^{i}M_{K}
for all
i
, there exist
i

with
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{i}M_{K}\neq{\rm Fil}^{i}M_{K}
, and
(M,{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})
is acyclic.
Indeed the degree of
(M,{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})
is then strictly smaller than that
of
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
. Hence by repeating the process one ends up with
a filtration such that
{\rm Fil}_{n}^{i}M_{K}\subset{\rm Fil}^{i}M_{K}
for all
i
,

(M,{\rm Fil}_{n}^{\bullet})
is acyclic and of degree
0
, hence it is of
Harder-Narasimhan slope
0
, i.e., it is weakly admissible.

To construct such a
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet}
, let
M^{1}
be the largest subobject
of Harder-Narasimhan slope
0
, and let
M^{2}
be the quotient
M/M_{1}
.
Then
M^{2}
has Harder-Narasimhan slope
>0
, and if we pick up any filtration

{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet}
on
M^{2}_{K}
, such that
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{i}M^{2}_{K}={\rm Fil}^{i}M^{2}_{K}
for
all
i
except
i_{0}
, for which
{\rm Fil}^{i_{0}}M^{2}_{K}/{\rm Fil}^{i_{0}}_{1}M^{2}_{K}
is of
dimension
1
, then
(M^{2},{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})
has
Harder-Narasimhan slope
\geq 0
since the degree of any subobject
has decreased by at most
1
and hence is
\geq 0
.
Then defining
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{i}M_{K}
as the
inverse image of
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{i}M^{2}_{K}
in
{\rm Fil}^{i}M_{K}
gives a filtration
with the desired properties.

The converse implication is obvious: the Harder-Narasimhan slope of
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})

is greater or equal to that of
(M,{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})
.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
be an acyclic filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module over
K
,
with
\varphi
-slopes in
[0,r]
, and
{\rm Fil}^{0}M_{K}=M_{K}
,
{\rm Fil}^{r+1}M_{K}=0
.
Then there exists a filtration
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet}
on
M_{K}

such that
(M,{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})
is weakly admissible and

{\rm Fil}_{1}^{i}M_{K}\subset{\rm Fil}^{i}M_{K}
for all
i
,

{\rm Fil}_{1}^{0}M_{K}=M_{K}
,
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{r+1}M_{K}=0
.

Proof.

In the proof of Lemma
5.3
, the
\varphi
-slopes of
M^{2}
are in
[0,r]
, and since

\deg(M^{2})>0
, this implies that
{\rm Fil}^{1}M_{K}\neq 0
. Let
{i_{0}}
be the largest integer
with
{\rm Fil}^{i_{0}}M_{K}\neq 0
; define
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet}
by
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{i_{0}}M_{K}

of codimension
1
in
{\rm Fil}^{i_{0}}M_{K}
, and
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{i}M_{K}={\rm Fil}^{i}M_{K}
is
i\neq 0
.
Then, as in the proof of Lemma
5.3
,

(M,{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})
is acyclic,

{\rm Fil}_{1}^{0}M_{K}=M_{K}
,
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{r+1}M_{K}=0
, and
\deg(M,{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})<\deg(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
. Iterating the process gives the wanted filtration.
∎

5.1.3.
The complex attached to a filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module

If
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
is a filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module over
K
, we set

X_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}):=(M\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})^{N=0,\varphi=1},\quad X_{\rm dR}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}):=(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})/{\rm Fil}^{0}(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}).

Then
X_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
and
X_{\rm dR}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
are
inductive limits of the
X^{(r)}_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})

and
X^{(r)}_{\rm dR}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
(defined by replacing
{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
and
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
by

t^{-r}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+}
and
t^{-r}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
),
which are objects of
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
.

The complex
X_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})\to X_{\rm dR}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})

is called
[
15
, § 5.3]
the “fundamental complex associated to
M
”.
Its
H^{0}
is denoted by
V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
. Hence, we have
an exact sequence

0\to V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})\to X_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})\to X_{\rm dR}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})

Remark 5.5
.

(i) The cohomology of the fundamental complex is equal
to that of the complex

X^{(r)}_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})\to X^{(r)}_{\rm dR}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
, for

r
big enough. It follows that its cohomology groups are objects of
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
.

(ii) The pair
(X_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}),X_{\rm dR}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}))

is a
B
-pair in the sense of Berger
[
4
,
5
]
; attached to it is a
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant
vector bundle
{\mathscr{E}}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
on the Fargues-Fontaine curve
[
22
, § 10.1]

and the fundamental complex computes the cohomology (not the
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant)
of this vector bundle. Since the HN-slope of
{\mathscr{E}}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
is
that of
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
, the vector bundle
{\mathscr{E}}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
has vanishing

H^{1}
if and only if
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
is acyclic (see Theorem
3.9
and formulas (
3.10
)). It follows that,
if
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
is acyclic, we have an exact sequence

0\to V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})\to X_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})\to X_{\rm dR}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})\to 0

(iii) If
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
is acyclic, then
V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})

is a finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space if and only if
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
is
of slope
0
(i.e., is weakly admissible). This implies it

is admissible
: the natural maps give
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant isomorphisms

\displaystyle V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})\otimes{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}\overset{\sim}{\to}M\otimes_{F}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}

of
{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
-modules commuting with
\varphi
and
N
,

\displaystyle V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})\otimes{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}\overset{\sim}{\to}M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}

of filtered
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
-modules,

and
V:=V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
is a potentially semi-stable representation of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
, with

D_{\rm dR}(V)=M_{K}
and
D_{\rm pst}(V)=M
.

(iv) If
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
is acyclic, but not admissible, the
natural map

V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})\otimes{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\to{\rm Fil}^{0}(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})

is not an isomorphism (the kernel is huge), but it is surjective. Indeed, one can pick a filtration

{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}_{1}
on
M_{K}
, such that
{\rm Fil}^{i}_{1}M_{K}\subset{\rm Fil}^{i}M_{K}

for all
i
, and
(M,{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})
is admissible.
Then we have an exact sequence

(5.6)

0\to V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})\to V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})\to{\rm Fil}^{0}(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})/{\rm Fil}_{1}^{0}(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})\to 0

and
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\cdot V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})

contains
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\cdot V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})
which,
by admissibility of
(M,{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})
,
is equal to
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{0}(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})

and the above exact sequence gives the desired surjectivity.

5.2.
Filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-modules and almost
C
-representations

5.2.1.
The functors
D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
and
D_{\rm st}^{\boldsymbol{*}}

The definitions of the classical contravariant functors
D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
,

D_{\rm st}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
and
D_{\rm pst}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
for finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-representations
of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
extend to objects of
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
contrarily to those
of the (more commonly used) covariant functors
D_{\rm dR}
,

D_{\rm st}
and
D_{\rm pst}
.
If
W\in{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
, set

D_{\rm st}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(W):={\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(W,{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}),\quad D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(W):={\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(W,{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}).

Then
D_{\rm st}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(W)
is a
(\varphi,N)
-module
14
14

14

Finite dimensionality comes from the
fact that
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(C,{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})\hookrightarrow{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(C,{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})
and the last
group is
0
by point (iv) of Proposition
2.14
. This reduces finite dimensionality
for
W\in{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})
to the case of finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-representations.

over
K
(with

\langle\varphi(\mu),v\rangle=\varphi(\langle\mu,v\rangle)
and

\langle N(\mu),v\rangle=N(\langle\mu,v\rangle)
)
and
D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(W)
is a filtered module over
K
(with

{\rm Fil}^{i}D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(W)=\{\mu,\ \mu(W)\subset t^{i}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\}
).
We also define
D_{\rm pst}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(W)
as:

D_{\rm pst}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(W):={\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}^{\rm sm}(W,{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}):=\varinjlim_{[L:K]<\infty}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{L}}(W,{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})

This is a
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module over
F^{\rm nr}
.

Remark 5.7
.

The natural maps

D_{\rm st}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(W)\otimes_{F}K\to D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(W),\quad(D_{\rm pst}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(W)\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}\overline{K})^{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}\to D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(W)

induced
by the injections
{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}\otimes_{F}K\hookrightarrow{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}

and
{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}\overline{K}\hookrightarrow{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
are
injective.

The following result is an extension to acyclic filtered
(\varphi,N)
-modules
of a classical result for admissible filtered
(\varphi,N)
-modules.

Theorem 5.8
.

If
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
is an acyclic filtered
(\varphi,N)
-module over
K
,
then

\displaystyle D_{\rm st}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}))\simeq M^{\boldsymbol{*}}

as
(\varphi,N)
-modules over
F
,

\displaystyle D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}))\simeq(M_{K}^{\boldsymbol{*}},{\rm Fil}_{\perp}^{\bullet})

as filtered
K
-modules.

Proof.

Let
V:=V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
.
We start by noticing that, thanks to point (iv) of Remark
5.5
, the natural pairing
injects
M^{\boldsymbol{*}}
into
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(V,{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})
(as a
(\varphi,N)
-module)
and
M_{K}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
into
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(V,{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})
.
Moreover, the natural map from
K\otimes_{F}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(V,{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})

to
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(V,{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})
is injective (see Remark
5.7
),
and one can deduce that
D_{\rm st}^{\boldsymbol{*}}\to M^{\boldsymbol{*}}

is an isomorphism from the same result for
D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
.

Hence, we just have to prove the result for
D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
. To do so,
pick a filtration
{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet}
as in (iv) of Remark
5.5
:

{\rm Fil}^{i}_{1}M_{K}\subset{\rm Fil}^{i}M_{K}
for all
i
and
(M,{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})

is admissible. Let
V:=V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
,
V_{1}:=V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}_{1}^{\bullet})
,
and
W={\rm Fil}^{0}(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})/{\rm Fil}_{1}^{0}(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})
, so that
the exact sequence (
5.6
) becomes
0\to V_{1}\to V\to W\to 0
.

Now,
W
is a direct sum
of factors of the form
t^{k_{1}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k_{2}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
. Hence

D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(W)=0
thanks to Proposition
2.14
,
and we get injections

M_{K}^{\boldsymbol{*}}\hookrightarrow D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(V)\hookrightarrow D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(V_{1})
.
But
\dim_{K}D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(V_{1})\leq\dim_{{\bf Q}_{p}}V_{1}=\dim_{K}M_{K}
(the first inequality is true
for any finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-representation of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
and the second equality is true because

V_{1}
is de Rham).
It follows that these injections are in fact isomorphisms, which proves what we
want except for the equality of the filtrations on
M_{K}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
and
D^{\boldsymbol{*}}_{\rm dR}(V)
.

So let
\mu\in M_{K}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
. One can extend
\mu
to a
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
-linear map

{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}\otimes_{K}M_{K}\to{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
. Thanks to (iv) of Remark
5.5
,
one sees that
\mu(V)\subset t^{i}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
is equivalent to

\mu({\rm Fil}^{0}(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}))\subset t^{i}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
.
But
{\rm Fil}^{0}(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})=\sum_{n}{\rm Fil}^{n}M_{K}\otimes_{K}t^{-n}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
,
and
\mu(M_{K})\subset K
, hence
\mu({\rm Fil}^{0}(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}))\subset t^{i}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}

if and only if
\mu({\rm Fil}^{n}M_{K})=0
for
n\geq 1-i
. By definition this
translates into
\mu\in{\rm Fil}^{i}_{\perp}M_{K}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
, as wanted.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

If
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
is an acyclic filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module over
K
,
then

\displaystyle D_{\rm pst}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}))\simeq M^{\boldsymbol{*}}

as
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-modules over
F^{\rm nr}
,

\displaystyle D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}))\simeq(M_{K}^{\boldsymbol{*}},{\rm Fil}_{\perp}^{\bullet})

as filtered
K
-modules.

Proof.

As in the proof of the above theorem,
we have an injection of the right-hand sides into the left-hand sides,
and to check that these are isomorphisms, we just have to bound the dimensions of the left-hand sides.
This can be achieved by passing to a finite extension
L
of
K
such that the action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{L}
on

M
is unramified, and applying the proposition to a
(\varphi,N)
-module
M(L)

over
F_{L}
such that
M=F^{\rm nr}\otimes_{F_{L}}M(L)
.
∎

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})

be an acyclic filtered
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-module over
K
, with
\varphi
-slopes in
[0,r]
,
and
{\rm Fil}^{0}M_{K}=M_{K}
,
{\rm Fil}^{r+1}M_{K}=0
.
Set

V^{r}_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}):={\rm Ker}\big((M\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}\to(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})/{\rm Fil}^{r}\big)

Then
15
15

15

The notations
M\{r\}
and
{\rm Fil}_{\perp}^{\bullet}\{r\}

mean that the action of
\varphi
is multiplied by
p^{r}
and that the filtration
is shifted by
r
: we have
{\rm Fil}_{\perp}^{i}\{r\}={\rm Fil}_{\perp}^{i-r}
.

\displaystyle D_{\rm pst}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(V^{r}_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}))\simeq M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\{r\}

as
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-modules over
F^{\rm nr}
,

\displaystyle D_{\rm dR}^{\boldsymbol{*}}(V^{r}_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}))\simeq(M_{K}^{\boldsymbol{*}},{\rm Fil}_{\perp}^{\bullet}\{r\})

as filtered
K
-modules.

Proof.

The conditions imply that
V^{r}_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})=t^{r}V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
.
Hence

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(V^{r}_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}),{\bf B}_{?})=t^{r}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(V_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}),{\bf B}_{?})

and the result follows.
∎

Example 5.11
.

If
M
in Corollary
5.10
satisfies
{\rm Fil}^{r}M_{K}=M_{K}
,
then
(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})/{\rm Fil}^{r}=0
. Hence

V^{r}_{\rm st}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})=X^{r}_{\rm st}(M):=(M\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}

The corollary then becomes:

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}^{\rm sm}(X^{r}_{\rm st}(M),{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})\simeq M^{\boldsymbol{*}},\quad{\text{as $(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})$-modules over $F^{\rm nr}$,}}

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(X^{r}_{\rm st}(M),t^{j}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})\simeq\begin{cases}M_{K}&{\text{if $j\leq 0$,}}\\
0&{\text{if $j\geq 1$.}}\end{cases}

Lemma 5.12
.

Under the hypothesis of Corollary
5.10
,
we have

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(V_{\rm st}^{r}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet}),C(j))=0
, for
j\geq r+1
.

Proof.

We can write
V:=V_{\rm st}^{r}(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})
in the form

0\to V_{1}{\to}V\to W\to 0
, as in the proof of Theorem
5.8
,
with
V_{1}
of dimension
{\rm dim}(M)
, de Rham with Hodge-Tate weights in
[0,r]
,
and
W
a finite type
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module, sum of

t^{a}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{b}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
’s, with
0\leq a\leq b\leq r+1
.

We have an exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(W,C(j))\to{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(V,C(j))\to{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(V_{1},C(j))

Since
j\geq r+1
, by Lemma
2.10
, we have

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(t^{a}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{b}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+},C(j))=0
if
b\leq r+1
; hence

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(W,C(j))=0
.
Since the Hodge–Tate weights of

V_{1}
are
\leq r
, we also have
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{G}}_{K})}(V,C(j))=0
.

This concludes the proof.
∎

5.3.
Filtered
(\varphi,N)
-modules over
C

5.3.1.
Filtered
(\varphi,N)
-modules and vector bundles

A filtered
(\varphi,N)
-module
(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})
over
C

is a pair
(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})
, where:

(1)

M
is a
(\varphi,N)
-module over
F^{\operatorname{nr}}
;

(2)

M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}\subset M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{\operatorname{dR}}
is a
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-lattice.

To such an
(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})
one can attach a vector bundle
{\mathscr{E}}(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})
on
X_{\rm FF}
, characterized by

H^{0}(X_{\rm FF}\mathbin{\fgebackslash}\{\infty\},{\mathscr{E}}(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}))=(M\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})^{N=0,\varphi=1},\quad{\mathscr{E}}(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{\rm FF},\infty}=M_{\rm dR}^{+}.

We set

{V}_{\rm st}(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}):=H^{0}(X_{\rm FF},{\mathscr{E}}(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}))={\rm Ker}\big((M\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})^{N=0,\varphi=1}\to(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{\operatorname{dR}})/M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}\big)

Definition 5.13
.

(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})
is
weakly admissible
if

{\mathscr{E}}(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})
is semistable, of slope
0
.
It is
acyclic
if the slopes
of
{\mathscr{E}}(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+})

are
\geq 0
.

Remark 5.14
.

(i) If
(M,{\rm Fil}^{\bullet})

is a weakly admissible (resp. acyclic) filtered
(\varphi,N)
-module over
K
,
the induced filtered
(\varphi,N)
-module
(M\otimes_{F}F^{\rm nr},{\rm Fil}^{0}(M_{K}\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}))

over
C
is weakly admissible (resp. acyclic).

(ii) It follows from the classification of vector bundles on
X_{\rm FF}
that

(M,M_{\rm dR}^{+})
is weakly admissible
if and only if it is admissible (i.e.,
{\mathscr{E}}(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+})
is
a direct sum of trivial line bundles
{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{\rm FF}}
).
This translates into the following:
(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})
is weakly admissible
if and only if
{V}_{\rm st}(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})
is finite dimensional over
{\bf Q}_{p}
and
the sequence

0\to{V}_{\rm st}(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})\to(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})^{N=0,\varphi=1}\to(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})/M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}\to 0

is exact. Moreover, if this is the case, the triviality of
{\mathscr{E}}(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})

implies that the natural maps

{V}_{\rm st}(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}\to M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}},\quad{V}_{\rm st}(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\to M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}

are isomorphisms (for the second map this is also equivalent to
the map
{V}_{\rm st}(M,M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}\to M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}

being a filtered isomorphism).

(iii) The following conditions are equivalent:

\bullet

(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+})
is acyclic,

\bullet

H^{1}(X,{\mathscr{E}}(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}))=0
(and hence
{\mathscr{E}}(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+})
is acyclic),

\bullet

(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})^{N=0,\varphi=1}\to(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})/M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}

is surjective.

\bullet
There exists a sub-
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
lattice
N_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}\subset M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}

such that
(M,N_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+})
is weakly admissible.

(The first two points are equivalent by Theorem
3.9
and formulas (
3.10
),
the second and the third are
equivalent because

H^{1}(X,{\mathscr{E}}(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}))={\rm Coker}\big((M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})^{N=0,\varphi=1}\to(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})/M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}\big)

the first
and last points are equivalent by the same arguments as in Lemma
5.3
.)

Remark 5.15
.

Since
(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+})\mapsto{\mathscr{E}}(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+})
commutes with tensor products, and since the
slope of
{\mathscr{E}}\otimes{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}
is equal to the sum of slopes of

{\mathscr{E}}
and
{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}
, the tensor product of two acyclic filtered

\varphi
-modules is again acyclic.

Remark 5.16
.

Assume that the
\varphi
-slopes are in
[0,r]
and

M\otimes t^{r}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\subset t^{r}M_{\rm dR}^{+}\subset M\otimes{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

\bullet

(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+})
is acyclic,

\bullet

(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}\to(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})/t^{r}M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
is surjective,

\bullet
for all
k\geq 0
,

(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}t^{-k}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}\to(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}t^{-k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})/t^{r}M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
is surjective.

This follows from the fact that
\frac{(M\otimes t^{-k}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}}{(M\otimes t^{1-k}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}}\simeq\frac{M\otimes t^{-k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}{M\otimes t^{1-k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}
, for
k\geq 1
,
as can be shown, for exemple, by a Dimension of BC argument (we have an injective natural map
from the left to the right that extends to a morphism of BC’s of the same Dimension
(\dim M,0)
:
the computation of the Dimension of the term on the right is immediate, that of the term on the left
follows from the Dimension computations of
[
16
, Ex. 5.18]
; it follows that this is an isomorphism of BC’s).
For the same reasons,
for all
k\geq 0
,

t^{r}{V}_{\rm st}(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+})={\rm Ker}\big((M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}t^{-k}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}\to(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}t^{-k}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})/t^{r}M^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}\big)

5.4.
Filtered
(\varphi,N)
-modules and BC’s

The following computations supply key arguments in the proofs of our pro-étale-to-de Rham comparison theorems in Chapter
9
.

5.4.1.
(\varphi,N)
-modules

The following results supply key arguments
in the proof of our main comparison theorem (Theorem
8.1
).

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
M
be a
(\varphi,N)
-module over
F^{\rm nr}
whose
\varphi
-slopes are in
[0,r]
.
Then the
{\bf Q}_{p}
-module
(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}
generates the
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module

M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
.

Proof.

Since
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
is a local ring, with residue field
C
,
it suffices to show that

(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}
generates the
C
-module

M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}C
.

Now, the map
x\mapsto x-u\,Nx+\frac{u^{2}}{2!}\,N^{2}x-\frac{u^{3}}{3!}\,N^{3}x+\cdots
,
for
u\in{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}}
maping to
\log([p^{\flat}]/p)
in
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
, induces
a
\varphi
-equivariant isomorphism
M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+}\to(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0}
.
Since
u
has image
0
in
C
, we are reduced to proving that

(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+})^{\varphi=p^{r}}
generates the
C
-module

M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}C
.

Since
{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+}
contains
W(k_{C})
, the theorem of Dieudonné-Manin allows us to reduce to the case
where
M
is elementary with slopes
\frac{a}{h}\leq r
, i.e., it is generated
by
e_{1},\dots,e_{h}
with

\varphi(e_{1})=e_{2},\,\varphi(e_{2})=e_{3},\dots,\,\varphi(e_{h})=p^{a}e_{1}.

The map
x\mapsto xe_{1}+p^{-r}\varphi(x)e_{2}+\cdots+p^{-(h-1)r}\varphi^{h-1}(x)e_{h}

induces an isomorphism of
U_{h,rh-a}:=({\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+})^{\varphi^{h}=p^{rh-a}}
with

(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+})^{\varphi=p^{r}}
. Hence we are reduced to proving that the image of

U_{h,rh-a}
by the map
x\mapsto(\theta(x),\theta(\varphi(x)),\dots,\theta(\varphi^{h-1}(x)))

does not lie inside a proper sub-
C
-module of
C^{h}
.
Assume that it does. Then there exists
\lambda_{0},\dots,\lambda_{h-1}\in C
, not all zero,
such that
\lambda_{0}\theta(x)+\lambda_{1}\theta(\varphi(x))+\cdots+\lambda_{h-1}\theta(\varphi^{h-1}(x))=0

for all
x\in U_{h,rh-a}
. In particular,
one can apply this to
\alpha x
, for
\alpha\in{\bf Q}_{p^{h}}
.
Setting
\mu_{i}(x)=\lambda_{i}\theta(\varphi^{i}(x))
, we get

\mu_{0}(x)\alpha+\mu_{1}(x)\varphi(\alpha)+\cdots+\mu_{h-1}(x)\varphi^{h-1}(\alpha)=0
,
for all
\alpha\in{\bf Q}_{p^{h}}
. Linear independence of characters implies that

\mu_{i}(x)=0
for all
i
and
x
, and we get our contradiction.
∎

Proposition 5.18
.

Let
M
be a
(\varphi,N)
-module with
\varphi
-slopes in
[0,r]
,
and let

{\mathbb{X}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M):=(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}.

Then:

{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{X}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})\simeq M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}},\quad{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{X}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm st}})\simeq M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}

Proof.

{\mathbb{X}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M)
is of curvature
\leq 0
,
and the conditions on the slopes imply (
[
16
, Ex. 5.18]
) that

{\rm ht}({\mathbb{X}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M))={\rm rk}(M)
.
It follows from Proposition
3.27
that
{\rm rk}(h({\mathbb{X}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M)))={\rm rk}(M)
.
On the other hand, the inclusion
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm st}}^{+}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}
induces a natural map

M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}\to h({\mathbb{X}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M))
.
Lemma
5.17
implies that this map is injective. Since the two modules have the same rank
over the field
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
, this natural map is an isomorphism, which provides our first isomorphism.

For the second isomorphism, the injection of the right-hand side into the left-hand side is obvious (it follows, for example, from the first isomorphism).
To prove the converse inclusion, it is enough, granting the first isomorphism,
to show that if
\lambda\in M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
satisfies

\lambda({X}_{\operatorname{st}}^{r}(M))\subset{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
, then
\lambda\in M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
.
For this, pick a weakly admissible filtration
M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}
on
M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}
, and
let
V:=V_{\operatorname{st}}(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+})
so that

V\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}\simeq M\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
(see Remark
5.14
).
Since
t^{r}V\subset X_{\operatorname{st}}^{r}(M)
, we have
\lambda(V)\subset{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
, and
since
V
generates
M\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
, we have
\lambda(M)\subset{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
.
This implies
\lambda\in M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
, as wanted.
∎

Remark 5.19
.

The map
x\mapsto x-\frac{u}{1!}Nx+\frac{u^{2}}{2!}N^{2}x-\frac{u^{3}}{3!}N^{3}x+\cdots

induces isomorphisms

\displaystyle{\mathbb{X}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M)\overset{\sim}{\to}{\mathbb{X}}_{\rm cris}^{r}(M):=(M\otimes{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm cris}}^{+})^{\varphi=p^{r}},\quad{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{X}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm cris}}^{+})\overset{\sim}{\to}{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{X}}_{\rm cris}^{r}(M),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm cris}}^{+})

Since
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm st}}={\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm cris}}^{+}[u,\frac{1}{t}]
, we obtain:

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{X}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm st}})=

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{X}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm cris}}^{+})\otimes_{{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}

\displaystyle M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+}\subset\

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{X}}_{\rm cris}^{r}(M),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm cris}}^{+})\subset M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}t^{-r}{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+}

(For the inclusion on the right, use the fact that
V
in the proof
of Proposition
5.18
is included in
t^{-r}X^{r}_{\rm cris}(M)

and
M\subset V\otimes{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+}
.)

5.4.2.
Filtered
(\varphi,N)
-modules

Proposition 5.20
.

Let
(M,M_{\rm dR}^{+})
be an acyclic filtered
(\varphi,N)
-module
over
C
with
\varphi
-slopes in
[0,r]
,
with
t^{r}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\otimes M\subset t^{r}M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}\subset{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\otimes M
.
Set
{\mathbb{M}}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}:={\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\otimes_{{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}
and

{\mathbb{V}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M,M_{\rm dR}^{+}):={\rm Ker}((M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}\to(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})/t^{r}{\mathbb{M}}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}).

Then

{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{V}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M,M_{\rm dR}^{+}),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})\simeq M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}},\quad{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{V}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M,M_{\rm dR}^{+}),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm st}})\simeq M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\rm nr}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}

Proof.

Let
{\mathbb{X}}_{\rm dR}^{r}(M):=(M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+})/t^{r}{\mathbb{M}}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
;
this is a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module killed by
t^{r}
.
The hypotheses give us an exact sequence

0\to{\mathbb{V}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M,M_{\rm dR}^{+})\to{\mathbb{X}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M)\to{\mathbb{X}}_{\rm dR}^{r}(M)\to 0

The first isomorphism is then a consequence of Proposition
5.18
and vanishing
of
{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{X}}_{\rm dR}^{r}(M),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})
and

{\rm Ext}^{1,\natural}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{X}}_{\rm dR}^{r}(M),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}})

(Corollary
3.18
and Lemma
3.26
).

For the second isomorphism pick up a weakly admissible filtration
N_{\rm dR}^{+}
containing

M_{\rm dR}^{+}
(this is possible because
(M,M_{\rm dR}^{+})
is acyclic),
set
V:={V}_{\rm st}(M,N_{\rm dR}^{+})
, and argue as in Proposition
5.18
.
∎

Remark 5.21
.

As in Remark
5.19
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{V}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm st}})=

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{V}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm cris}}^{+})\otimes_{{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}

\displaystyle M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+}\subset\

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({V}_{\rm st}^{r}(M,M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm cris}}^{+})\subset M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}t^{-r}{\bf B}_{{\rm cris}}^{+}

Remark 5.22
.

We could also have argued as in the proof of Theorem
5.8

to prove Propositions
5.18
and
5.20
.

Proposition 5.23
.

Let
(M,M_{\rm dR}^{+})
be an acyclic filtered
(\varphi,N)
-module
over
C
with
\varphi
-slopes in
[0,r]
, and such that

M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}t^{r}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\subset t^{r}M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}\subset M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
.
Let
k\geq 2r
. Then the natural map of
{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-modules

M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})\to{\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{V}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M,M_{\rm dR}^{+}),{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})

induced by the inclusion
{\mathbb{V}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M,M_{\rm dR}^{+})\subset M\otimes{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
,
has kernel and cokernel killed by
t^{2r}
.

Proof.

Choose a
N_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}
with
M\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}\subset N_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}\subset M_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}

such that
(M,N_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+})
is weakly admissible.
(This is possible by an adaptation of Lemma
5.4
to
(\varphi,N)
-modules over
C
.)
Let
V_{1}={\mathbb{V}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M,N_{\rm dR}^{+})
,
V={\mathbb{V}}_{\rm st}^{r}(M,M_{\rm dR}^{+})
,
and
W=V/V_{1}
, so that
V_{1}
is a finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space,
and
W=t^{r}{\mathbb{M}}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}/t^{r}{\mathbb{N}}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}
is a
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
-module killed by
t^{r}
.

Set
h_{k}(-):={\rm Hom}_{\rm VS}(-,{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})
.
Since (we skipped the subscripts
F^{\operatorname{nr}}
)

M\otimes\tfrac{t^{r}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}{t^{k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}\subset\tfrac{t^{r}{\mathbb{N}}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}}{M\otimes t^{k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}\subset\tfrac{t^{r}{\mathbb{M}}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}}{M\otimes t^{k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}\subset M\otimes\tfrac{{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}{t^{k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}

and
V_{1}\subset\tfrac{t^{r}{\mathbb{N}}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}}{M\otimes t^{k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}},

V\subset\tfrac{t^{r}{\mathbb{M}}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}}{M\otimes t^{k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}
,
we have a commutative diagram

where the composed map
M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})\to M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes(t^{r}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})
is
multiplication by
t^{r}
.
Now, since
V_{1}
is finite dimensional over
{\bf Q}_{p}
,
and
{\mathbb{N}}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}=V_{1}\otimes t^{-r}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}
, we have, using Proposition
3.17
,

h_{k}(\tfrac{t^{r}{\mathbb{N}}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}}{M\otimes t^{k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}})={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}}(\tfrac{t^{r}{\mathbb{N}}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}}{M\otimes t^{k}{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}},{\mathbb{B}}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})=V_{1}^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})=h_{k}(V_{1}).

It follows that:

\bullet

{\rm Ker}\big(M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})\to h_{k}(V)\big)
is a subobject
of
{\rm Ker}(M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})\overset{t^{r}}{\to}M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes(t^{r}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}))
,
hence is killed by
t^{r}
.

\bullet

{\rm Coker}(h_{k}(V)\to h_{k}(V_{1}))
is a subquotient
of
{\rm Coker}(M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})\overset{t^{r}}{\to}M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes(t^{r}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k}))
,
and hence is killed by
t^{r}
.
The cokernel of
M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})\to h_{k}(V_{1})

is also killed by
t^{r}
and, since the kernel of
h_{k}(V)\to h_{k}(V_{1})
is
h_{k}(W)

which is killed by
t^{r}
, it follows that
{\rm Coker}(M^{\boldsymbol{*}}\otimes({\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}^{+}/t^{k})\to h_{k}(V))

is killed by
t^{2r}
.

This concludes the proof.
∎

6.
Comparison theorems: examples and a conjecture

In this Chapter we will formulate a conjecture: the existence of the fundamental diagram for smooth dagger varieties over
C
. Before doing that though we will first look at examples of comparison theorems and fundamental diagrams.

6.1.
Cliffs Notes

Here we make a small digression with a quick
review of relevant results from
[
17
]
and
[
18
]
.

Proposition 6.1
.

(Colmez-Nizioł,
[
17
, Th. 1.1]
,
[
18
, Th. 1.3]
)

(1)

Analytic varieties
:
To any smooth dagger or rigid analytic variety
X
over
C
there are naturally associated:

(a)

A pro-étale cohomology
{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\in{\mathscr{D}}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})
,
r\in{\bf Z}
.

(b)

A syntomic cohomology
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{syn}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\in{\mathscr{D}}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})
,
r\in{\bf N}
, with a natural period morphism in
\in{\mathscr{D}}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})

\alpha_{r}:{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{syn}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r)),

which is a strict quasi-isomorphism after truncation
\tau_{\leq r}
. This morphism can be lifted to the derived category of Topological Vector Spaces.

(c)

A Hyodo-Kato cohomology
16
16

16

We take here the Hyodo-Kato cohomology defined in
[
18
]
.

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\in{\mathscr{D}}_{\varphi,N}(F^{\rm nr})
. We have natural Hyodo-Kato strict quasi-isomorphisms in
{\mathscr{D}}(C_{C})
,
{\mathscr{D}}(C_{{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}})
, respectively:

\iota_{\operatorname{HK}}:{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\widehat{\otimes}^{R}_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(X),\quad\iota_{\operatorname{HK}}:{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\widehat{\otimes}^{R}_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}).

(d)

A distinguished triangle in
{\mathscr{D}}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{syn}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits}[{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\widehat{\otimes}_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}}]^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\iota_{\operatorname{HK}}}}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{r}

that can be lifted to the derived category of Topological Vector Spaces.

(e)

(Local-global compatibility)

In the case
X
has a semistable weak formal model the above constructions are compatible with their analogs defined using the model.

(2)

Compatibility
: Let
X
be a smooth dagger variety over
C
and let
\widehat{X}
denote its completion. Then there exist natural compatible morphisms
[
17
, Sec. 3.2.4]

\displaystyle\iota_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}:\

\displaystyle{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\widehat{X},{\bf Q}_{p}(r)),\quad r\in{\bf Z},

\displaystyle\iota:\

\displaystyle{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{syn}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{syn}}(\widehat{X},{\bf Q}_{p}(r)),\quad r\in{\bf Z},

\displaystyle{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(\widehat{X}),\quad{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(\widehat{X}/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}),

\displaystyle{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(\widehat{X}).

They are strict quasi-isomorphisms, preserving all the extra structures, if
X
is partially proper.

6.2.
Proper rigid analytic varieties

We start with smooth and proper varieties.

6.2.1.
Algebraic varieties

Let
X_{K}
be an
algebraic
variety over
K
and set
X=X_{K,\overline{K}}
. Recall the comparison theorem (recall that all the cohomology groups involved have finite dimension):

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
r\geq 0
.
There exists a natural
{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
-linear Galois equivariant period isomorphism
17
17

17

Note that there is no assumption on the variety. This formulation of the comparison theorem is due to Beilinson
[
3
]
.

\alpha_{\operatorname{st}}:H^{r}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}\simeq H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}},

that preserves the Frobenius and the monodromy operators, and induces a filtered isomorphism

\alpha_{\operatorname{dR}}:H^{r}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}\simeq H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{K})\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}.

In particular, we have the natural isomorphism

H^{r}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq(H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})^{\varphi=1,N=0}\cap F^{0}(H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{K})\otimes_{K}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}),\quad\text{as a ${\mathscr{G}}_{K}$-module,}

as well as the natural isomorphisms

(6.3)

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}^{\rm sm}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(H^{r}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}),{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})\simeq H^{r}_{{\rm HK}}(X)^{\boldsymbol{*}},\quad{\text{as a $(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})$-module,}}

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(H^{r}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}),{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})\simeq H^{r}_{{\rm dR}}(X_{K})^{\boldsymbol{*}},\quad{\text{as a filtered $K$-module}}.

6.2.2.
Proper rigid analytic varieties over
K

Let
X_{K}
be a proper smooth rigid analytic variety over
K
. Let
X=X_{K,C}
. The following result generalizes
[
16
, Cor. 1.10]
, where semistable reduction was assumed.
We note that all involved cohomology groups have finite dimension: for étale cohomology this is the result of Scholze
[
43
, Th. 1.1]
; for Hyodo-Kato cohomology this follows from the Hyodo-Kato isomorphism and finiteness of de Rham cohomology.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
r\geq 0
.
There exists a natural
{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
-linear and
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant period isomorphism

\alpha_{\operatorname{st}}:H^{r}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}\simeq H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}},

that preserves the Frobenius and the monodromy operators, and induces a filtered isomorphism

\alpha_{\operatorname{dR}}:H^{r}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}\simeq H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR},\overline{K}}(X)\otimes_{\overline{K}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}.

In particular, have the following natural isomorphisms

(6.5)

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}^{\rm sm}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(H^{r}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}),{\bf B}_{{\rm st}})\simeq H^{r}_{{\rm HK}}(X)^{\boldsymbol{*}},\quad{\text{as a $(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})$-module,}}

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{G}}_{K}}(H^{r}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}),{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}})\simeq H^{r}_{{\rm dR}}(X)^{\boldsymbol{*}},\quad{\text{as a filtered $K$-module}}.

Proof.

Take
s>r
. To define the period maps consider the following composition

\displaystyle\alpha_{\operatorname{st}}(s):H^{r}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(s))\xrightarrow[\sim]{\alpha_{s}^{-1}}H^{r}_{\operatorname{syn}}(X,s)\to(H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}^{+})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{s}}\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{p^{-s}}}H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}},

where
\alpha_{s}
is the period map from
[
18
, Cor. 6.9]
.
Set

\alpha_{\operatorname{st}}:=t^{-s}\alpha_{\operatorname{st}}(s)\varepsilon^{s},\quad\alpha_{\operatorname{dR}}:=\iota_{\rm BK}^{-1}(\iota_{\operatorname{HK}}\otimes\iota)\alpha_{\operatorname{st}},

where
\varepsilon
is the generator of
{\bf Z}_{p}(1)
corresponding to
t
.

We follow the proof of
[
16
, Cor. 1.10]
, which uses
BC’s. We will sketch the arguments and refer the reader to
[
16
]
for details. We start with stating the isomorphism

(H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)\otimes_{K}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(H^{r}({\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)\otimes_{K}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{s}),

which follows from the degeneration of the Hodge-de Rham spectral sequence
18
18

18

Alternatively, one can use an argument analogous to the one we use in the proof of Theorem
6.8
below.
(proved by Scholze as a corollary of the de Rham comparison theorem
[
43
]
). Using this isomorphism, we obtain
the long exact sequence (obtained from the definition of overconvergent syntomic cohomology)

(6.6)

\displaystyle\to(H^{r-1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)\otimes_{\overline{K}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{s}

\displaystyle\to H^{r}_{\operatorname{syn}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(s))\to(H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{s}}

\displaystyle\to(H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR},\overline{K}}(X)\otimes_{\overline{K}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{s}\to

We recall that the groups
H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X),H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)
have finite dimension over
F^{\operatorname{nr}}
and
K
, respectively.
The above long exact sequence yields short exact sequences

0\to H^{r}_{\operatorname{syn}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(s))\to(H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{s}}\to(H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR},\overline{K}}(X)\otimes_{\overline{K}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{s}\to 0

To prove this, we observe that the map

f_{r}:(H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{s}}\to(H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR},\overline{K}}(X)\otimes_{\overline{K}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{s}
is the evaluation on
C
of a map
of BC’s. But, the syntomic cohomology group
H^{r}_{\operatorname{syn}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(s))
,
r\leq s
,
is a finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space since
we have the quasi-isomorphism
[
18
, 6.10]
with étale cohomology.
This implies that the cokernel of
f_{i}
, viewed as a map of BC’s,
is of Dimension
(0,d_{i})
.
On the other hand, the Space
(H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR},\overline{K}}(X)\otimes_{\overline{K}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{s}
is a successive extension of
C
-vector spaces.
The theory of BC’s implies now that the map
(H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR},\overline{K}}(X)\otimes_{\overline{K}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{s}\to{\rm Coker}\,f_{r}
is zero, hence
{\rm Coker}\,f_{r}=0
,
as wanted.

We have the Hyodo-Kato isomorphism (see
[
18
, Cor. 4.32]
)

\iota_{\operatorname{HK}}:H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}C\simeq H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X).

Taking
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-smooth vectors of both sides (note that
X
is quasi-compact) we get the Hyodo-Kato isomorphism

H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}\overline{K}\simeq H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{K})\otimes_{K}\overline{K}.

Hence the pair
(H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X),H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{K}))

is a
(\varphi,N,{\mathscr{G}}_{K})
-filtered module (in the sense of Fontaine). The above short exact
sequence and a “weight" argument shows that
{{\mathbf{V}}_{\operatorname{st}}}(H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X),H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{K}))\simeq H^{r}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})
. Here
{{\mathbf{V}}_{\operatorname{st}}}(-)
is Fontaine’s functor from filtered Frobenius modules to Galois representations.
The short exact sequence and
C
-dimension count give also that
t_{N}(H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X))=t_{H}(H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{K}))
, where
t_{N}(D)=v_{p}(\det\varphi)
and
t_{H}(D)=\sum_{i\geq 0}i\dim_{K}(F^{i}D/F^{i+1}D)
.
The theory of BC’s now implies that the pair
(H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X),H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{K}))
is weakly admissible from which our theorem follows.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

The isomorphisms (
6.3
) and (
6.5
) are strict if we put the natural
topology on the
{\rm{Hom}}
’s.

6.2.3.
Proper rigid analytic varieties over
C

Let
X
be a smooth and proper rigid analytic variety over
C
.
Its
p
-adic étale cohomology is of finite rank by
[
43
, Th. 1.1]
.
Its Hyodo-Kato cohomology is of finite rank by the Hyodo-Kato isomorphism and finiteness of de Rham cohomology.
Its
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-cohomology is free of finite rank over
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
, by the comparison isomorphism with Hyodo-Kato cohomology.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
r\geq 0
.
There exists a natural
{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}
-linear period isomorphism

\alpha_{\operatorname{st}}:H^{r}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}}\simeq H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\otimes_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm st}},

that preserves the Frobenius and the monodromy operators, and induces a filtered isomorphism

(6.9)

\alpha_{\operatorname{dR}}:H^{r}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}\simeq H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})\otimes_{{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}}{\bf B}_{{\rm dR}}.

Here, the filtration on
H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})
is defined by

F^{i}H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}):=\operatorname{Im}(H^{r}(F^{i}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}))\to H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})).

Remark 6.10
.

A de Rham comparison isomorphism as in (
6.9
) was constructed earlier in
[
7
, Th. 13.1]
.
It did not treat filtrations.

Proof.

Take
s>r
. The period maps are defined as in Theorem
6.4

but by dropping the map
\iota_{\rm BK}
. We note that

H^{r}({\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{s})
is a BC, a successive extension of
C
-vector spaces of finite rank. This follows from the fact that the distinguished triangle

[
18
, 3.28]
yields the distinguished triangle
